# Issues found on the live site — sixthsensemma.com

Compiled 2026-09-15 while rebuilding the site. Every issue below was checked
against the live pages, not taken from notes. Counts come from scanning the
live HTML of **104 pages**: the homepage, the 5 program pages and all 98 blog
posts.

Each issue is tagged:

- **SECURITY** — the site or its visitors are at risk
- **SEO** — hurts how the site ranks or appears in search results
- **CONTENT** — wrong, broken or confusing for a visitor
- **GEOGRAPHIC TARGETING** — content aimed at the wrong location for a local business

The rebuilt site already fixes or works around every issue here, with one
exception: issue 0 is a claim the rebuild **omits** rather than reproduces, and
it needs a decision from the client before that page can go live as published.
The client also still needs to act on the live WordPress site, most urgently
issue 0, then issues 1 and 2, then issue 40, which is now sending link equity to
a competing gym.

---

## Most urgent — act on this before anything else

### 0. EIGHTEEN posts promise an absence of injury risk, two of them on classes sold to five-year-olds, two on weapons training with no minimum age and one that teaches elbow strikes to the temple and offers home practice — CONTENT (accuracy), LIABILITY — **URGENT**

#### A fourth page that needed no omission

`/benefits-of-kickboxing/` (8 March 2026, 1,923 words) required **no omission**.
"Concussion" and "brain" appear zero times, and so do "without risk", "low risk",
"injury free" and every equivalent. The one risk sentence is a purpose clause and
was kept: "They know how to teach proper technique for punches kicks and
combinations so you **avoid injury** and get maximum power."

**Its FAQ names the costs of the sport**, like `/adult-muay-thai-classes-near-me/`
did. FAQ 10, "Is kickboxing hard on your body?": "**Shins take repeated impact
which can cause bruising or soreness. Joints like knees ankles and shoulders get
stressed** from twisting kicking and punching. Hands can hurt if you hit without
good wraps or gloves." Kept verbatim.

Four of the last five builds have needed no omission. All four are adult Muay
Thai or kickboxing pages written in 2026; the eighteen pages that did need them
remain concentrated in the kids, weapons and neck-bridge groups.

#### A third page that needed no omission

`/history-of-muay-thai/` (8 March 2026, 3,561 words) required **no omission**.
"Concussion" and "brain" do not appear; the only risk language is historical and
comparative — "Officials supervised matches **to prevent serious harm**" (about
19th-century regulators), "These changes made Muay Thai **safer** and more
professional", "gear helped **prevent fractures**". All kept verbatim.

The closing Contact section is a class pitch, which is where these pages usually
slip, and it makes no safety claim at all.

#### A second page that needed no omission

`/benefits-of-muay-thai/` (7 March 2026, 2,814 words) is a benefits page for a
striking sport — the category most likely to overstate safety — and **not one
sentence required omission**. The words "concussion" and "brain" do not appear;
neither do "without risk", "low risk", "injury free" or any equivalent. The nine
uses of "safe" are all ordinary ("keeping yourself safe", "feel safer walking
alone", "clean and safe facilities").

**It also makes no health claim that needed checking against a clinician**, which
is worth recording given what the kids and women's pages contained. See the note
under issue 62.

Two of the last three builds have now needed no omission at all. Both are adult
Muay Thai pages. The eighteen pages that did need omissions are concentrated in
the kids, weapons and neck-bridge groups.

#### A page that needed no omission, and names the risk itself

`/adult-muay-thai-classes-near-me/` (27 February 2026, 3,938 words) is a striking
page teaching elbows, knees and clinch work, and **not one sentence required
omission**. Every risk statement is qualified: "You practice with partners to feel
pressure **safely**", "Instructor enforce strict safety rules controlled sparring
proper warm ups and constant supervision **to prevent injuries**", "keep contact
**light to moderate**", "stop rounds **instantly if needed**", "the focus stays on
learning **safely** and having fun".

**More than that, its FAQ is the only place on the entire site where the business
names concussion as a risk of its own product.** FAQ 10, "What are the negatives
of Muay Thai?", answers in full:

> Frequent impact causes bruises, shin conditioning pain and **joint wear**
> especially knees, elbows and hands. Hard sparring or fights lead to cuts black
> eyes and **occasional concussions**. Training is very intense so **overtraining
> fatigue or burnout** happens if you push too much without rest. The high
> physical demands can strain the body long term leading to **arthritis or chronic
> pain later in life** … **Injury risk exists** especially without good coaching
> or protective gear.

Set against the sixty-four omitted sentences above, this is what the client should
be shown as the model. The same business that told readers neck bridges are not
risky and that children cannot be hurt has, on this page, published an honest
list of the downsides of its own class. **Nothing here was altered.** The
recommendation is to move this answer out of the accordion and into the body, and
to use its tone on the other seventeen pages.

#### The eighteenth page, three sentences, and the worst context in the whole issue

`/mma-classes-for-kids/` (26 February 2026) publishes **three** sentences that
have been **omitted from the rebuild**, each leaving an HTML comment:

> They learn brazilian jiu jitsu moves bjj holds and ways to grapple **without
> getting hurt**.

> This setup lets children practice striking grappling submission and technique
> **without real danger** while they build skill and confidence.

> Strict rules no full contact unless ready proper tap signals and constant
> supervision **prevent any issues**.

**This is a page selling mixed martial arts to four-year-olds**, and the second
of the three sits directly under the heading "Protective Gear and Controlled
Sparring", in the paragraph that describes children sparring in headgear. "Without
real danger" is the claim a parent reads immediately after being told their child
will wear a mouthguard and a chest protector. The third, "prevent any issues", is
a flat guarantee that nothing will go wrong.

**The first omission costs some fidelity and is flagged for that reason.** Cutting
it also removes "They learn brazilian jiu jitsu moves bjj holds", which is
technique content. The sentence could not be split without rewriting the client's
words, so the whole sentence went and this note records what was lost. The fix at
source is three words: "… and **safe ways to grapple**."

**The page's own FAQ is more honest than its body, and all of it was kept:**

| Where | Exact wording |
|---|---|
| FAQ 3 | "**Risks exist like minor injuries if contact gets too hard or supervision lacks** but good youth programs **limit head strikes**, use protective gear, control sparring and prioritize technique over power" |
| FAQ 3 | "Compared to some youth sports MMA often shows **lower serious injury rates** when done properly" — comparative, not absolute |
| Body | "These steps **keep injuries very low**" — very low, not none |
| Body | "This equipment absorbs impact cushions hits and **reduces any chance** of bumps or bruises" |
| Body | "never face **unnecessary risk**" |
| Body | "include built in steps **to prevent injuries**" — purpose clause |

**Note what the FAQ says and the body does not.** FAQ 3 states that good youth
programmes "**limit head strikes**". The body of the page never mentions head
strikes at all — it describes children throwing "clean punches" and "kicks", and
teenagers doing "full contact style drills", without once saying where the strikes
may land or that strikes to the head are limited. For a kids MMA page that is the
single most important sentence missing, and the page's own FAQ supplies it.

**No concussion or brain-injury claim appears**, in either direction: the words
"concussion" and "brain" do not occur anywhere on the page.

**Running total: 64 sentences across eighteen posts.**

#### The seventeenth page, and one of the two is attached to clinch sparring

`/muay-thai-classes-coppell/` (26 February 2026) publishes **two** sentences that
have been **omitted from the rebuild**, each leaving an HTML comment:

> You practice with partners to feel real pressure **without danger**.

> Coach adjust intensity and focus so beginner feel comfortable and fighter get
> challenged **without risk**.

**The first is in the clinch section**, directly after "you learn to control an
opponent up close grab neck sweep throw knees and stay balanced". Partner clinch
work with knees is the single highest-contact thing this page describes, and the
sentence says it carries no danger. "Without danger" is the same wording already
omitted from `/muay-boran-techniques/` and `/anti-bullying-martial-arts-for-kids/`,
so this is its third appearance.

**The second is in the closing section on programme structure** and covers
everyone from beginner to fighter, including the "tougher sparring" the same
paragraph advertises.

**The page gets it right four other times, and all four were kept verbatim:**

| Where | Exact wording |
|---|---|
| Controlled sparring | "Sessions stay technical not wild **to avoid injury** and help you read opponents better" |
| Controlled sparring | "full combos **while staying protected**" |
| Facility | "Coach enforce strict safety rules controlled sparring proper warm ups and constant supervision **to prevent injuries**" |
| Fight prep | "We cover everything from **cutting weight safely** to peaking on fight day" |

So the page knows how to write the qualified version — it does so four times — and
then writes the unqualified version twice. Both fixes are one word.

**One sentence was considered and kept**, and the reasoning is recorded because it
goes the other way from a similar call: "Try Muay Thai Classes Coppell **with no
risk** through our free trial class." On `/muay-boran-techniques/` the comparable
sentence was cut, because its object was "muay boran **techniques** risk free" — a
claim about the training. Here the object is the *trial*, the phrase sits inside a
heading called "Free Trial Muay Thai Class in Coppell", and the sentences around
it are about cost and commitment ("**No pressure to sign up** right then"). It
reads commercially, so it stays. The client may still prefer "**at no cost**",
which removes the ambiguity entirely.

**Notably, there is no concussion or head-trauma claim anywhere on the page.** For
a striking article that teaches elbows and head kicks, the words "concussion" and
"brain" do not appear at all — better than `/types-of-sambo/`, which required four
omissions on exactly that ground.

**Running total: 61 sentences across seventeen posts.**

#### The sixteenth page, and the promise is about adults and children on the mat together

`/family-martial-arts-classes/` (26 February 2026) publishes **one** sentence that
has been **omitted from the rebuild**, leaving an HTML comment:

> Instructors watch closely enforce proper form and **make sure no one gets hurt**
> or feels left out.

**The context is what makes this one different.** The sentence sits in "Safe
Supportive and Fun Environment for Families", immediately after "rules that keep
everyone protected during punch kicks and punches grappling and partner work" —
and the partner work this page describes is **a parent and a child paired
together** (see issue 88). "Make sure no one gets hurt" is therefore a promise
about mixed-age contact training, which is the one context where it is least
defensible.

It is also the same wording already omitted from
`/brazilian-jiu-jitsu-coppell-tx/` ("so no one gets hurt from ego or rushing"), so
the phrase is now a template habit rather than a one-off.

**Everything else on the page is qualified and was kept verbatim**, including
"rules that **keep everyone protected**" and, in the FAQ, "Good instructors modify
classes, use chairs for support and emphasize safety **to prevent injury**" — a
purpose clause, correctly phrased.

**Running total: 59 sentences across sixteen posts.**

#### The fifteenth page, and the word is now "harmless"

`/kids-self-defense-classes-near-me/` (26 February 2026) publishes **two**
sentences that have been **omitted from the rebuild**, each leaving an HTML
comment:

> Kids wear headgear mouthguards shin guards gloves chest protectors and padding
> sized just for them so **any contact stays light and harmless**.

> This level of attention makes sure training stays safe positive and effective so
> kids build self confidence self discipline and real skills **without risk**.

**"Harmless" is a stronger word than anything in the previous fourteen pages.**
The others said training carried no risk; this one says the contact itself cannot
harm a child. It is also the first time the claim is attached to **protective
equipment** — the sentence lists six pieces of gear and then concludes that contact
is harmless, which is precisely the reasoning head-injury research has spent
twenty years correcting. Padding reduces harm; it does not remove it.

**"Without risk" is the plainest form of the claim yet** and sits in the section
about instructor supervision, so it reads as a promise about the staff.

**Two neighbouring sentences were kept**, because both are about fear rather than
injury and the project has consistently kept that distinction: "staying protected
and learning to stay calm **without fear of getting hurt**" and "Instructors
enforce strict rules light contact only proper protective gear and no rough play
so kids train **without fear of getting hurt**".

**Running total: 58 sentences across fifteen posts.**

#### The fourteenth page, and the first one written for parents of bullied children

`/anti-bullying-martial-arts-for-kids/` (26 February 2026) publishes **two**
sentences that have been **omitted from the rebuild**, each leaving an HTML
comment:

> Parents want their child to learn self defense build confidence and handle
> bullying **without any risk of getting hurt**.

> This controlled pace helps children develop good habits **without danger**.

**The first needs a note, because its grammar hides what it does.** Read strictly
it reports what parents *want*, not what the gym delivers. But it opens the
"Safety Measures" section and the next sentence is "**That is why** martial arts
programs here follow strict rules", which answers the want — so a parent reads it
as a promise that their child will not be hurt. It has been cut on that reading.
If the client disagrees, the fix is one word: "**with as little risk as possible**
of getting hurt". The sentence is reproduced here in full so the client can judge
for themselves.

**The second is the same "without danger" formulation already omitted from**
`/muay-boran-techniques/`, and it is attached to drills for four-year-olds.

**Everything else on the page is qualified and was kept verbatim**, including the
correct version of the same claim two sections later: "Every class follows clear
rules so kids don't face **unnecessary risks**" — which is exactly how both omitted
sentences should have been written.

**Running total: 56 sentences across fourteen posts.**

#### The thirteenth page, three sentences, and a heading that makes the same claim

`/neck-bridges-benefits/` (22 January 2026) publishes **three** sentences that have
been **omitted from the rebuild**, each leaving an HTML comment:

> Neck bridges **aren’t risky** when done right; **they prevent injuries** by
> conditioning the area to handle pressure.

> Progression at Sixth Sense MMA moves deliberately **from zero risk** to high
> challenge.

> Fighters appreciate the immediate corrections that keep sessions productive and
> **injury free**.

**The first is the most serious sentence in this entire issue.** It is on a page
about loading the cervical spine with full body weight, and it says the exercise
is not risky and that it prevents injuries. Two paragraphs of the same page
describe "axial compression and shear forces directly through the head and neck",
disc "degeneration bulging or even herniation", and "nerve roots causing radiating
pain numbness tingling down the arms or weakness in the hands". The page contains
its own refutation, in more clinical detail than any other page on the site.

**A fourth instance is a heading, and it has been reproduced.** The H3
**"Injury Free Training System"** makes the same claim as the omitted sentences,
in two words, above the fold of its section. It has been **kept verbatim** for two
reasons: the omission rule is written for sentences, and removing a heading would
break the document outline, the table of contents and the heading-by-heading
comparison that proves the rebuild is faithful. **It is logged here instead, and it
needs changing at source** — "Safe Training System" or "Injury Prevention System"
would carry the same meaning without the absolute claim. Every sentence underneath
that heading is properly qualified, which makes the heading the only problem in the
section.

**The rest of the page is the most risk-honest writing the client has published.**
These were all kept verbatim:

| Where | What it says |
|---|---|
| H2 | "**Risks of Neck Bridges Without Professional Guidance**" |
| H3 | "**Common Injuries from Wrong Technique**" |
| H3 | "**Cervical Disc & Nerve Damage**" |
| H3 | "**Why Supervised Training Is Important**" |
| Body | "People with past neck injuries neck problems or limited mobility need extra caution and **often medical approval first**" |
| Body | rehab plans "for whiplash cervical disc issues or post surgical recovery **when cleared by a doctor**" |
| FAQ 1 | "However, **improper form or overtraining can lead to injury**, so beginners should practice carefully" |
| FAQ 2 | "it may also **cause strain or injury if overdone**" |
| FAQ 10 | "More intense exercises like neck bridges should include rest days **to avoid strain or injury**" |

So this page both over-promises and under-promises, in different sections. The fix
is small: delete three sentences, rename one heading, and the page becomes the
model the other two neck posts should follow.

**Running total: 54 sentences across thirteen posts.**

#### The twelfth page, and this one is contradicted by its own FAQ

`/brazilian-jiu-jitsu-coppell-tx/` (22 January 2026) publishes **one** sentence
that has been **omitted from the rebuild**, leaving an HTML comment in its place:

> We teach proper tapping controlled speed and awareness of partners **so no one
> gets hurt** from ego or rushing.

**The page refutes itself eight paragraphs later.** Its own FAQ answers "What Are
the Downsides of Jiu-Jitsu?" with:

> Downsides of Jiu-Jitsu include **the risk of injuries (especially joint and neck
> injuries)**, the time required to achieve proficiency, plateaus in skill
> progression, and the physical intensity which can be challenging for beginners
> or older practitioners.

and "Why Do Most People Quit BJJ?" with "Many people quit BJJ due to factors like
the physical intensity of training, slow progress, **injuries**, time
commitments…". Both FAQ answers are reproduced unchanged — they are the honest
version, and they are on the same page as the claim that no one gets hurt.

**This is the clearest internal contradiction in the whole safety group.** The
other eleven posts overstate safety without ever admitting a risk. This one
admits joint and neck injuries explicitly, in its own words, and still opens the
methodology section by promising nobody is hurt. A client reading only this entry
should understand the fix is to delete seven words: "We teach proper tapping
controlled speed and awareness of partners **to keep training safe**."

**Nine other risk sentences on the page are correctly qualified and were kept
verbatim**, including "This approach builds efficient habits that save energy and
**reduce injury risk**", "Instructor watch every roll closely and **stop unsafe
movements immediately**", and "This safety first mindset lets people train hard
**without fear**" (about fear, not injury).

**One borderline sentence was kept and is flagged here:** "Our instructor keeps
classes energetic **and safe** so every child feels included and excited to
return." It is a descriptive present-tense claim rather than a guarantee, so it
stays — but it is on the kids section of a page that also claims no one gets hurt,
and the client may want to qualify it.

**Running total: 51 sentences across twelve posts.**

#### The eleventh page, and the first one where the guarantee is attached to the instructors

`/adult-beginner-martial-arts/` (22 January 2026) publishes **one** sentence that
has been **omitted from the rebuild**, leaving an HTML comment in its place:

> Their experience in bjj muay thai boxing mma and self defense **ensures lessons
> stay practical and safe**.

**This one is a different shape from the other ten.** The previous ten denied that
a technique or a class carries risk. This one does not mention risk at all — it
guarantees an outcome ("safe") and attributes the guarantee to named credentials.
That is harder to defend, not easier: it tells a reader that the instructors'
experience is itself the assurance, so if someone is hurt the claim points
straight at the staff.

**The rest of the page is the best safety writing on the site, which is why this
sentence stands out.** Every other risk sentence on the page is correctly
qualified, and all of them are reproduced unchanged:

| Sentence | Why it is fine |
|---|---|
| "Instructor teach proper falling tapping out and listening to your body so **risk stays low**" | risk reduction, not denial |
| "light spar that builds skill **without high risk**" | qualified |
| "Classes start with thorough warm ups mobility work and joint preparation **to reduce risk**" | risk reduction |
| "the system supports long term progress **without shortcuts or unnecessary risks**" | qualified, the correct form |
| "Adult Beginner Martial Arts is **generally safe when** taught by experienced instructors" (FAQ 9) | conditional |

The page even names the fear honestly — an H3 called "Fear of Injury" that opens
"Many adults worry that martial arts will lead to sprains strains or worse". A
page willing to write that does not need the one unqualified sentence, and it is
a one-word fix: "**helps keep** lessons practical and safe".

**Two further sentences were considered and kept.** Neither denies risk, but both
are worth the client's eye:

- "This safety first mindset lets adults train consistently **without fear of
  setbacks**" — about fear, not about injury, so it stays.
- "Many report feeling stronger and more durable over time because **the system
  protects joints** builds resilience and teaches smart movement" — framed as what
  students report, so it stays, but "the system protects joints" read as a flat
  assertion would belong in the list above.

**Running total: 50 sentences across eleven posts.**

#### The tenth page, and one of the two sentences is attached to a free trial

`/muay-boran-techniques/` (20 January 2026) publishes two sentences that have
been **omitted from the rebuild**, each leaving an HTML comment:

> Practice happens at slow speed first then adds light resistance to build feel
> **without danger**.

> Trial classes let newcomers try muay boran techniques **risk free**.

The second is worth separating out. "Risk free" attached to a **trial class** may
have been meant commercially — no money at risk — but it is not written that way:
the sentence is "try muay boran **techniques** risk free", and the techniques in
question are the ones the same page describes as "powerful elbow strike to the
temple", "arm dislocation technique" and "heel kick to thigh or leg causing
dislocation". Read plainly it promises a reader they can try those at no risk.

If the intent was commercial, the fix is two words — "with **no cost or
commitment**" — and it removes the liability entirely. That ambiguity is worth
flagging to the client rather than guessing, which is why the sentence was cut
rather than reworded.

**The first one has the same shape as the weapons page's.** "Build feel without
danger" appears in the clinch-and-takedown section, where students are being
thrown. The same page writes the correct version twice — "This careful approach
lets anyone build skill **without unnecessary risk**" and "Takedowns stay
controlled with emphasis on **landing safely**" — both reproduced unchanged, both
properly qualified.

**Running total: 49 sentences across ten posts.**


#### The ninth page, two sentences, and one of them is about a safety RULE

`/mix-martial-arts-for-self-defense/` (20 January 2026) publishes two sentences
that have been **omitted from the rebuild**, each leaving an HTML comment:

> Men learn precise control that **avoids wild swings and injury risks**.

> Group energy motivates more and **safety rules prevent injury**.

The second is the more quotable of the two. "Safety rules prevent injury", stated
flat in the present tense, is the claim a gym cannot make: rules reduce injury,
they do not prevent it, and the sentence appears in a passage recommending
professional classes over home practice — exactly where a reader is weighing the
risk of turning up.

**The distinction the page itself gets right elsewhere.** Three screens earlier
it writes "Safe & practical techniques for women prioritize moves that avoid
prolonged struggle and **reduce injury risk**" — "reduce", correctly hedged, and
reproduced unchanged. The same page therefore contains both the right phrasing
and the wrong one, which makes the correction a copy-and-paste from one of its
own paragraphs to the other two.

**Everything else stays**, including "Training stays controlled with emphasis on
tapping early and partner cooperation", "**Coach supervision stays constant**
during every drill", "Ukemi rolls proper breathing and **injury prevention**
build trust", and the whole of the beginner-progression passage.

**Running total: 47 sentences across nine posts.**


#### The eighth page, and the phrase is now "without risk" — twice, about weapons

`/martial-arts-weapons-training/` (20 January 2026) publishes two sentences that
have been **omitted from the rebuild**, each leaving an HTML comment, and both use
the same phrase:

> We use safe traditional practice weapons made of wood and foam to teach proper
> technique **without risk**.

> This controlled approach lets students practice weapons forms combination
> attacks and defensive moves **without risk**.

The second is the worse of the two, because of what it is attached to. The
"combination attacks and defensive moves" in question are the ones the same page
describes as "linking strike spin block and flail movements with nunchaku
longsword blade and stick" — swinging a wooden longsword at a partner. "Without
risk" is not a thing a gym can promise about that, and the sentence sits under the
H3 "**Strict Safety Standards and Professional Equipment**", where a cautious
reader goes looking.

**The first is nearly defensible and shows the shape of the fix.** Wood and foam
practice weapons genuinely do reduce the risk — that is why they exist. "To teach
proper technique **with far less risk**" would be true, would say the same thing,
and is a three-word edit.

**Everything else on the page stays**, and there is a lot of it: "Safety comes
first in every class", "All practicing weapons happens **under close supervision
to prevent injury**", "Students always follow clear rules and use proper gear",
"Spar starts only when ready and every session **builds mastery the safe way**",
"keeping safety and control first". Those are risk-*reduction* statements and all
five are reproduced.

**Running total: 45 sentences across eight posts**, and this is the **second**
weapons page in the group — `/martial-arts-weapons-training-in-coppell/` supplied
three of the earlier ones.


#### The seventh page, three sentences — on a page written for complete beginners

`/sambo-techniques-for-beginners/` (20 January 2026) publishes three sentences
that have been **omitted from the rebuild**, each leaving an HTML comment:

> **Safe takedown drills build skill without danger for beginner students.**

> New students learn ukemi rolls right away so **landings never cause harm**.

> Sambo mixes high intensity bursts with controlled technique so heart and lungs
> improve **without joint strain**.

The audience is what makes these worse than the earlier six. This page is aimed
at people who have never trained, it teaches hip throws, ouchi gari, shoulder
lifts and heel hooks, and the first sentence appears under the H3 "**Safe
Takedown Drills**" — the exact place a nervous beginner looks for the risk
picture. "Without danger" and "landings never cause harm" are promises about
being thrown onto a mat.

**The surrounding copy is the best in the whole audit and must be kept.** The same
page says "Tapping out happens early and often during holds **to stop pressure
before it hurts**", "Sambo stresses clean technique and body awareness **to
prevent common injuries like twisted knees or strained shoulders**", "learns
proper falling techniques called ukemi right away **to land softly and avoid
harm**", and "finish the drop **without wild risks**". Those are
risk-*reduction* claims, correctly hedged, and all four are reproduced. Only the
three absolute denials were cut.

**The fix is three words each**: "with far less danger", "so landings are much
softer", "with less joint strain". The page already writes in exactly that
register everywhere else.

**Running total: 43 sentences across seven posts.**


#### The sixth page, one sentence — and the same page carries the only honest caveat on the site

`/best-martial-arts-for-self/` (30 December 2025) publishes one sentence that
has been **omitted from the rebuild**, with an HTML comment in its place:

> Safe gear and rules let push hard **without hurt**.

It sits under the H3 "Safe, Supportive, and Controlled Training Environment", so
it is the sentence a prospective member reads when they go looking for exactly
this reassurance. "Push hard without hurt" is a promise no gym can keep.

**But read the rest of the page before judging it**, because it does something
none of the other five do. In its opening section it states:

> **No martial art guarantees success but good ones build fighting ability
> through practice.**

That is the only sentence on any of the 94 blog posts that explicitly **refuses**
an outcome guarantee, and it is the closest thing to a disclaimer anywhere on the
site (issue 31). It is reproduced unchanged.

So this page both makes the fault and demonstrates the fix: the client already has
house wording for "we do not promise outcomes", in their own voice, on the same
page. Applying it to the injury sentence takes four words — "lower the risk of
injury" — and the correction for the other five posts is already written.

**Running total: 40 sentences across six posts**, 9 of them on classes for ages
5-12 and 3 on weapons training with no minimum age.


#### The fifth page, and the most serious: weapons training called "risk free"

`/martial-arts-weapons-training-in-coppell/` (26 December 2025) advertises bo
staff, escrima stick and katana classes, and promises an absence of injury three
times:

> **These rules make weapon training martial arts enjoyable and risk free.**

> These martial arts practice weapons allow full speed drills **without harm**.

> Foam or rubber versions of sticks swords and staffs allow full speed contact
> **without injury**.

All three have been **omitted from the rebuild**, each leaving an HTML comment in
its place. The rest of the page is reproduced verbatim.

**Why this one is worse than the other four.** The first sentence does not
qualify anything: it says that following the page's own safety tips makes weapons
training *risk free*. The second and third promise *full speed* contact with no
harm. The page is selling classes in which children swing wooden and padded
weapons at each other, it states **no minimum age anywhere**, and it carries **no
disclaimer of any kind** (issue 31). A parent reading "risk free" and "full speed
drills without harm" has been told, in the gym's own words, that there is nothing
to weigh.

**The surrounding copy is mostly fine and should be kept.** The same page says
"Enroll with awareness of these tips to maximize growth and minimize risks",
"blades teach edge control timing and respect for danger", "Certified instructor
stress control to avoid injury" and lists helmets, gloves, padded suits, mats and
marked areas. Those are risk-*reduction* statements and they are reproduced
unchanged. Only the three absolute denials were cut. **The fix is three
sentences**, not a rewrite: "lower the risk", "at controlled speed" and "with
padded contact" would say what the gym presumably means.

**One borderline sentence was kept and is flagged here rather than cut:** "It
allows full power spar and kata **without sharp risk**", about the wooden bokken.
Read literally that is true — a bokken has no edge — so it describes the
equipment rather than promising an injury-free session. A bokken can still break
bones, so the client may want to reword it, but it is not the same class of claim
as the three above.


`/types-of-sambo/`, in the paragraph under the H3 **"Safe, Controlled and
Professional Training Environment"**, publishes these four sentences:

> Concussion risk none. Brain injuries stay out. Brain damage no chance. Head
> trauma camp free.

This is a medical safety guarantee. It tells a prospective student — or a parent
reading on behalf of a child — that training at this gym cannot cause a
concussion, a brain injury or head trauma. No combat-sports gym on earth can
promise that, and the same page contradicts it: the paragraph these four
sentences sit inside also says "Head strikes helmet on", "Strikes to the head
glove catch", "Blows to the head ref call" and "Head shots body lead", and the
page elsewhere carries "Concussion real none", "Concussion beginner none",
"Brain injuries train smart" and "Head trauma performance safe" — the same claim
restated eight more times in degraded form.

It also contradicts the rest of the site. `/muay-thai-gear-for-safety/` exists
to tell readers that head protection matters and that gear is what keeps them
safe; `/mixed-martial-arts-training-gloves/` is a buying guide premised on
impact being real. One page sells protective equipment on the grounds that
strikes to the head are dangerous, and another promises that at this gym they
are not.

**Affected:** 1 page, `/types-of-sambo/`.

**Why it matters:** this is not a typo or an SEO problem. It is a written
representation about physical safety, made to consumers, by a business that
teaches striking to adults and children. If a student is concussed in a class at
this gym, this sentence is the first thing a lawyer will put in front of a jury,
and it is on the client's own domain in the client's own words. It is also the
kind of claim a regulator or a platform treats as a false health claim
regardless of intent.

**Status in the rebuild: OMITTED.** `dist/types-of-sambo.html` reproduces that
section's heading and every other sentence in it verbatim, and leaves an HTML
comment where the four sentences were:

```html
<!-- HELD: false head-injury safety claim, see client-site-issues.md,
     awaiting client decision before publication -->
```

Nothing was paraphrased, softened or substituted in their place — the gap is
literal, and the eight restatements elsewhere on the page are still there,
reproduced as published, because removing those is a content decision the client
has not been asked for yet.

### The same copy block is on a second post, and there it is 29 sentences

`/mixed-martial-arts-vs-kickboxing/` was published on **the same afternoon** as
`/types-of-sambo/` — 11 November 2025, 13:45 and 16:06 — and it carries **the
identical four sentences, word for word**, under an H3 called "Safe Sparring and
Rest Strategies":

> Head shots body first. **Concussion risk none. Brain injuries stay out. Brain
> damage no chance. Head trauma camp free.** Neck injuries tap quick.

That is not a coincidence. It is the same block of copy pasted into a second
article, which means this is a process fault, not a one-off mistake.

On that post the pattern runs much further. **29 sentences** across 24 distinct
forms deny concussion, brain-injury, brain-damage or head-trauma risk, or
guarantee safety against it:

| Section | Sentences published |
|---|---|
| H3 Warm-Up, Recovery, and Conditioning | "Concussion stays away." · "Brain trauma zero in drill." |
| H3 Safe Sparring and Rest Strategies | "Concussion risk none." · "Brain injuries stay out." · "Brain damage no chance." · "Head trauma camp free." |
| H2 Who Can Practice MMA or Kickboxing | "Concussion stays away." · "Brain injuries zero in class." · "Brain damage no risk." |
| H3 Beginners Looking to Build Fitness and Confidence | "Concussion zero chance." · "Brain trauma camp free." |
| H3 Athletes Interested in Self-Defense Training | "Concussion street none." |
| H3 Women and Teenagers Learning Combat Discipline | "Concussion zero risk." · "Brain trauma camp none." |
| H2 MMA and Kickboxing Offer at Sixth Sense MMA | "Brain injuries train out." |
| **H3 Kids MMA Programs** (ages 5-12) | **"Concussion zero risk." · "Brain injuries safe play."** |
| **H3 Kids Kickboxing Programs** (ages 5-12) | **"Concussion camp none." · "Brain injuries safe play." · "Brain damage zero chance." · "Head trauma kid free."** |
| H3 Teen &amp; Adult Kickboxing Programs | "Brain damage pro avoid." |
| H2 Why Choose SixthSenseMMA | "Brain injuries camp free." · "Brain damage no chance." · "Head trauma safe rules." |
| H2 Conclusion | "Concussion never here." · "Brain injuries train out." · "Brain damage no place." · "Head trauma safe home." |

**Nine of the 29 are on the two kids' sections**, under headings that name
"ages 5-12" and offer a free trial. "Brain damage zero chance" and "Head trauma
kid free" are addressed to the parent of a five-year-old, on a page about
kickboxing.

**Three things make the second post worse than the first.**

1. **It contradicts itself inside its own FAQ.** The body says concussion risk
   at this gym is "none". The FAQ, 500 words later, answers "Is MMA safer than
   kickboxing?" with *"Both MMA and kickboxing carry risks since they are
   full-contact combat sports"*, and "What is the safest combat sport?" with
   *"No combat sport is completely without risk."* The accurate statement is
   already on the page, directly beneath the false one. Whoever wrote the FAQ
   knew; whoever wrote the body did not.
2. **The claims are attached to bookable products.** Four programmes are named
   with ages (5-12, 13+), class length (60 minutes), a free trial offered five
   times and free open-mat days twice. `/types-of-sambo/` made a vague claim
   about a discipline; this one promises no brain damage in a class a parent can
   book on Monday. Three of those four programmes have no programme page behind
   them (issue 39).
3. **The page is explicitly about danger.** Its own title asks "Which is
   Dangerous", it cites the *Manuel Velazquez Boxing Fatality Collection*, and it
   publishes three tables grading brain-injury risk — one of them headed
   "Medical Insights from SixthSenseMMA's Trainers", presenting the gym's own
   coaches as the medical authority. A reader is told head trauma is real for 1,400
   words and then told it cannot happen here.

**Status in the rebuild: all 29 OMITTED.**
`dist/mixed-martial-arts-vs-kickboxing.html` reproduces every heading, table,
paragraph and FAQ answer verbatim and cuts only those 29 sentences, each replaced
in place by:

```html
<!-- OMITTED: false head-injury safety claim, see client-site-issues.md issue 0 -->
```

The rule applied was narrow and mechanical: a sentence goes only if its subject
is one of five medical outcomes (concussion, brain injuries, brain trauma, brain
damage, head trauma) **and** its predicate denies that outcome or guarantees
safety against it. Everything else on the same subjects stays, because it is
either true or merely a marketing claim rather than a medical one — "Concussion
hits hard", "Brain damage steals futures", "Repetitive head trauma builds cte",
"Head trauma lingers long", "Concussion watch close", "Concussion risk low",
"Concussion risk drops". **25 such sentences were kept.** Nothing was
paraphrased, softened or substituted; the gaps are literal.

### A third page, one sentence, on an exercise with documented injury risk

`/do-neck-bridges-build-muscle/` (16 November 2025) carries one sentence of the
same kind, in its "Exercise basic concept" section:

> Neck bridge lifts head off ground in bridge position. It loads cervical spine
> with body weight. [...] **Ways to build neck without risk start here.**

A neck bridge loads the cervical spine with bodyweight. There is no way to do it
without risk, and the same page says so twice — "they can also be risky if
performed incorrectly" and "Neck bridges the wrong way risks getting injured".
The sentence contradicts its own article three paragraphs later.

**This page is otherwise the best of the three on safety**, which is why only one
sentence was cut rather than 29. It tells the reader "Neck bridges aren't for all",
it says "Axial load tests herniation risk", and it repeats **"Medical clearance if
neck issues" four times**. Those are all reproduced. What it does not have is a
disclaimer (issue 31).

**Status in the rebuild: OMITTED.** `dist/do-neck-bridges-build-muscle.html`
reproduces everything else verbatim — every technique step, every anatomy claim,
every set and rep figure — and leaves a comment where that one sentence was:

```html
<!-- OMITTED: claims the neck can be built without risk, see client-site-issues.md issue 0 -->
```

### A fourth page calls a strike to the kidneys "safe", twice

`/are-kidney-shots-legal-in-ufc/` (17 November 2025) is about a strike that
damages an internal organ, and it says so clearly for most of its length — "There
is almost no muscle or bone protection there so the shock travels straight into
the internal organ", "internal bleeding laceration contusion and possible organ
failure". Then twice it calls the thing safe:

> This protects fighters from internal bleeding laceration contusion and possible
> organ failure **while still letting painful but safe kidney and liver punches
> stay in the game.**

> **These rules keep the sport intense yet safe** protect fighters from serious
> internal organ damage and reward smart precise body work.

"Painful but safe kidney punches" is the claim, in a sentence that has just
listed organ failure as the thing being avoided. The second does the same for the
sport as a whole. A reader who takes either at face value is being told that a blow
which the same page says travels straight into an unprotected kidney carries no
real risk.

A third instance is in the referee-decision table, in the last row:
**"Allowed — Safe and permitted in UFC/MMA."** That one is a cell label rather than
a sentence, so it is reproduced as published rather than cut, and the comparison
table stays whole — but the client should change it with the other two.

**Status in the rebuild: both sentences OMITTED.**
`dist/are-kidney-shots-legal-in-ufc.html` reproduces every other sentence verbatim
— the rules, the refereeing table, the fouls list, the symptom description — and
leaves a comment where each cut sentence was:

```html
<!-- OMITTED: downplays the medical seriousness of kidney trauma, see client-site-issues.md issue 0 -->
```

**Note what was lost with them.** Both sentences carried accurate content as well —
the first names internal bleeding, laceration, contusion and organ failure, the
second names serious internal organ damage. Cutting the whole sentence, as the rule
requires, removes that too. The page still describes the injury mechanism in detail
elsewhere, so nothing factual disappears from the article, but when the client
rewrites these two sentences the accurate halves are worth keeping.

**What to do about the pattern, not just the pages:** both posts were published
within three hours of each other from what is evidently the same copy source,
and both reuse the same `Types-of-sambo-*` image batch. Before fixing either
page, find out what produced them — a writer, an agency, or a tool — and check
every post published since 11 November 2025 for the same four sentences. Deleting
them twice solves nothing if the source is still running.

**What to do:** remove these four sentences from the live page immediately —
before the malware, because this one is a statement the business is answerable
for, and deleting it takes one edit. Then decide what the section should say
instead; if the intent was that sparring is controlled and head contact is
limited, that can be said truthfully. Then tell us whether to publish the
rebuilt page with the gap, with replacement copy you approve, or with the
original sentences restored. Until then the rebuilt page is accurate but
incomplete by design. The eight restatements elsewhere on the page need the same
decision.

## SECURITY

### 1. Hidden casino spam links injected into every page — SECURITY — Urgent

Every one of the 63 pages checked contains a block of links to French online
casino sites. It sits just inside `<body>`, above the header, in an element
pushed off-screen with `position:absolute; left:-35255px` so visitors never see
it. Search engines do.

Domains linked:
`shinywilds-fr.fr`, `blitzcasino.org`, `oscarspinfr.fr`, `quickwin-casino.app`,
`rizzcasino-fr.net`, `spin-million.com`, `cashedcasinoligne.fr`,
`fr-winmachancecasino.com`

**Affected:** all 63 pages checked — homepage, all 5 program pages, all 57 posts.

**Why it matters:** this is a hacked WordPress site. Hidden outbound links to
gambling sites are a classic SEO-spam injection and can get the site flagged or
penalised by Google. Whatever put it there may have other access too.

**What to do:** treat as a compromise, not a content fix. Scan the install for
malware; check the active theme (`header.php`, `functions.php`), all plugins,
and the database (`wp_options`, `wp_posts`) for the injected code; remove
unknown admin users; update WordPress core, theme and plugins; change every
password and the database credentials. Deleting the visible block alone will
not stop it coming back.

## CONTENT

### 2. Wrong phone number on every tap-to-call link — CONTENT — Urgent

**And the label-less contact block is now on two pages.**
`/martial-arts-weapons-training/` publishes the same three bare list items as
`/sambo-techniques-for-beginners/` below — no "Phone", no "Email:", no
"Location:", no bold and no no-break spaces — and the same sixth phone format,
"**(469) 972 7800**". Both are from the January 2026 batch, so the contact block
changed shape when the newer template came in. **In the rebuild** both pages
reproduce it exactly.

**A sixth format, and a contact block with no labels at all.**
`/sambo-techniques-for-beginners/` publishes the number as **"(469) 972 7800"** —
parenthesised area code, then **spaces** instead of a hyphen. That is a sixth
published form of the correct number, and it appears on no other page.

Its contact block is also structurally different from every other post. The other
94 publish three labelled list items with bold values and a no-break space:
"Phone <strong>469 972 7800</strong>", "Email:<strong> info@…</strong>",
"Location:<strong> 817 S MacArthur…</strong>". This one publishes three bare
items with **no labels, no bold and no no-break spaces**:

```
817 S MacArthur Blvd #100 Coppell TX 75019 United States
(469) 972 7800
info@sixthsensemma.com
```

So a screen-reader user hears three unlabelled strings, and the phone number is
not announced as a phone number. **In the rebuild** both the format and the
missing labels are reproduced exactly, and the `tel:` link in the page furniture
is the correct `+14699727800`. **What to do:** one contact block, used
everywhere, with labels.

**And now both formats on ONE page, four lines apart.**
`/sambo-techniques-in-coppell/` writes the number twice in its Contact section:
the paragraph says "**Call (469) 972-7800** or email info@sixthsensemma.com." and
the bulleted list immediately beneath it says "Phone **469 972 7800**". Same
section, same screen, two formats. Every other page picks one. **In the rebuild**
both are reproduced as published and the `tel:` link is the correct
`+14699727800`.

**A fifth way: parenthesised.** `/kickboxing-gloves/` publishes it as
**"(469) 972-7800"** — "Call (469) 972-7800 or email info@sixthsensemma.com." That
makes five published forms of the correct number across the blog: hyphenated,
hyphenated inside `<strong>` with the full stop inside the bold, space-separated,
bare digits in the `tel:` attribute, and now parenthesised. The rebuild reproduces
each page's own spelling and routes every `tel:` link to `+14699727800`.

**A fourth way of writing the right number.**
`/do-neck-bridges-build-muscle/` publishes it **space-separated** — "Phone: 469 972
7800" — where the rest of the site hyphenates it, "469-972-7800". That makes four
published forms of the correct number across the blog: hyphenated, hyphenated
inside `<strong>` with the full stop inside the bold (`/sambo-moves/`), bare
digits in the `tel:` attribute, and now space-separated. A space-separated number
is not auto-detected as dialable by some mobile browsers, so a reader on a phone
has to retype it. The rebuild reproduces the published spacing and routes every
`tel:` link to `+14699727800` regardless.

The tap-to-call links use `tel:4639727800`. The real number is
**(469) 972-7800**. The area code is wrong, 463 instead of 469, so anyone who
taps "call" on a phone dials a different number.

**Affected:** all 63 pages. It appears twice on the homepage and on each blog
post, and once on each program page.

**What to do:** change every link to `tel:+14699727800`. It is in the site-wide
header and footer, so it should be a single template edit.

the spelling.

### 12. AI-tool markup pasted into posts: ChatGPT, and now DeepSeek — CONTENT — Medium

**A third AI tool, and this one is inside a published link: `?referrer=grok.com`.**
`/sambo-techniques-in-coppell/` publishes its Contact paragraph email as:

```html
<a href="mailto:info@sixthsensemma.com?referrer=grok.com">info@sixthsensemma.com</a>
```

A `referrer=grok.com` parameter has been appended to the gym's own email link.
Nobody writes that by hand: it is the tracking parameter **Grok** adds to links
it generates, carried into the page along with the text and never cleaned up. It
is harmless to a reader — mail clients ignore an unknown mailto parameter — but
it is a third AI tool's fingerprint in the published source, after ChatGPT and
DeepSeek below, and it is the first one inside an attribute rather than in
markup.

**In the rebuild** the link is reproduced exactly, parameter included, because
changing where a published link points is not a reproduction. **What to do:**
strip `?referrer=grok.com` on the live site; the link works identically without
it. And it is worth searching the whole site for `referrer=` — if one link
carries it, others may.

Two passages were copied straight out of the ChatGPT website, bringing its page
code with them (`gizmo-bot-avatar`, `bg-token-main-surface-primary`). The text
reads normally, but the stray code wraps it and it shows exactly how the post
was drafted to anyone who views the source.

**Affected:** `/martial-arts-for-adults/`: the opening line of "Self-Defense
Skills and Personal Safety", and the whole Conclusion.

**Two quieter cases:** on `/muay-thai-gear/`, four of the eight FAQ answers, and
on `/muay-thai-vs-kickboxing/`, four of the seven, carry `data-start` and
`data-end` attributes. Those are added by ChatGPT's web interface when text is
copied from it — on the Kickboxing post they appear on `<strong>` tags inside
the answers as well. Nothing shows on the page, but it is the same copy-paste
route as above, on two consecutive posts, and it confirms the FAQ answers were
pasted straight from a chat window.

**A different AI tool, on `/martial-arts-gyms-near-me/`.** All five FAQ answers
on the site's newest post are wrapped in `<p class="ds-markdown-paragraph">`.
That class is **DeepSeek's** markdown renderer, not ChatGPT's — a different
assistant, the same copy-paste route, four months later.

The same five answers also carry the structural debris that comes with pasting
rendered markdown into a rich-text field:

| Answer | What came with it |
|---|---|
| 2 | An empty ordered list after the text: `<ol start="1"><li> </li></ol>` |
| 3 | The **entire answer** wrapped in a one-item `<ol>`, so it renders as a numbered "1." for no reason, followed by an empty paragraph |
| 4, 5 | A trailing empty `<p> </p>` |

Answer 3 is the visible one: a reader sees a numbered list of one item where the
other four answers are plain paragraphs.

**Why the second tool matters more than the first.** One post drafted in ChatGPT
is a shortcut. Two different assistants leaving their own markup across four
posts, five months apart, is the drafting process. Taken with the untranslated
"Ka" (issue 17) and the unreviewed headings across the "near me" set, the
picture is consistent: text is being generated elsewhere and pasted in without a
read-through.

**What to do:** re-paste those passages as plain text, and paste future answers
through a plain-text editor so the wrapper classes and empty list items are
stripped. Then delete the stray `<ol>` in FAQ answers 2 and 3 on
`/martial-arts-gyms-near-me/`.

**Affected:** 22 instances across 4 posts

| Post | Count |
|---|---|
| `/martial-arts-karate-gi/` | 8 |
| `/is-boxing-a-martial-art/` | 7 |

### 17. Spelling errors, garbled headings and one untranslated Hindi/Urdu word — CONTENT — Medium

**A page title that stops mid-phrase, and a no-break space inside a heading.**
`/muay-boran-techniques/` carries two faults of this kind.

**The `<title>` ends on a singular noun that should be plural, and reads as a
truncation:**

```
Muay Boran Techniques Traditional Thai Combat Moves & Strike
```

"Moves & Strike" is not a phrase. Either the writer meant "Moves & Strikes", or
the title was cut at a character limit mid-word — "Strikes", "Striking" or
"Strike Techniques" would all fit the sentence. Whichever it was, this is the
string Google prints in bold at the top of the result, so the first thing a
searcher reads about the page is a phrase that stops dead. It is the same class
of fault as the truncated slug at issue 44, one level up: there the URL was cut,
here the title is. **In the rebuild** it is reproduced exactly.

**And an H2 contains a no-break space where a normal space belongs:**

> "Why Choose Sixth Sense MMA\u00A0 for Muay Boran Training"

There are **two** characters between "MMA" and "for" — a no-break space followed
by an ordinary one — so the heading renders with a visible double gap. It is the
same stray-nbsp pattern already logged on `/10-muay-boran-techniques/` and
`/are-there-multiple-styles-of-muay-boran/`, which suggests it comes from
pasting out of a word processor. **In the rebuild** it is reproduced exactly,
because collapsing it would change the published text.

**A stray letter "B" left in the body where a heading was cut.** On
`/mix-martial-arts-for-self-defense/` the Krav Maga section's final paragraph
ends:

> "Sixth Sense MMA offers krav maga sessions that build fighting ability through
> realistic pressure so you walk away ready. **B**"

The next element is the H2 "**B**razilian Jiu Jitsu (BJJ) for Self Defense". So
the heading's first letter was typed into the paragraph above it and never
removed — the reader sees a sentence ending in a full stop, then a lone capital
B, then the heading. It renders as a one-letter paragraph fragment.

It is the same class of fault as the dangling "Learning sambo at Sixth Sense
Martial Arts ." below, and it is the kind of thing a single read-through catches.
**In the rebuild** it is reproduced exactly.

**A heading missing a space, a sentence missing its predicate, and a word cut in
half by a link.** `/sambo-techniques-in-coppell/` carries three separate faults
of this kind:

| What | As published | The problem |
|---|---|---|
| H2 | "Why Choose Sixth Sense for Sambo Techniques Training in **Coppell,TX**" | no space after the comma |
| Paragraph | "**Learning sambo at Sixth Sense Martial Arts .** Benefits of learning sambo cover body control and calm in chaos." | the opening sentence has no predicate — it stops after the business name — and the full stop is preceded by a **no-break space** |
| Link | "**Moves sambo mart**ial art teach leverage over strength" | the auto-linker wrapped "Moves sambo mart" and left "ial art" outside it, cutting the word **martial** in half |

The second is the worst: the first sentence under an H2 is an incomplete
fragment, so the section opens on a dangling phrase. The third is the first time
the auto-linker has split a word rather than a clause (issue 5). **In the
rebuild** all three are reproduced exactly, no-break space included.

**A misspelled section heading on the strongest local page.**
`/martial-arts-near-me-in-coppell-texas/` publishes its closing H2 as
**"Concluson"** — "Conclusion" with the second "i" missing. It is one of sixteen
H2s on the page, so it is among the largest text on screen, and it sits on the page
with the highest commercial intent in the project (issue 50). The rebuilt page
reproduces it rather than silently correcting it, and its heading anchor is
`#concluson` to match.

**The "Brands Brands" heading looks like a find-and-replace gone wrong.** On
`/mixed-martial-arts-brands/`, the heading reads "Mixed Martial Arts Brands
Brands vs Traditional Martial Arts Brands". Replacing "MMA" with the keyword
phrase "Mixed Martial Arts Brands" in "MMA Brands vs Traditional Martial Arts
Brands" would produce exactly this. It is worth checking other posts for
headings where a keyword phrase has been substituted in, since the same edit
would have run across the site.

**A word left in the wrong language entirely, on `/fight-training-near-me/`.**
One of its H3s reads:

> Physical Fitness + Mental Strength **Ka** Combination

"Ka" is the Hindi and Urdu possessive particle, roughly "of". The heading was
evidently composed as "Physical Fitness + Mental Strength **ka** Combination" —
half English, half Hindi/Urdu — and published without anyone reading it back. It
is not a typo, a brand name or a martial-arts term; it is a word from another
language sitting in an English heading on a US business's website.

**Why this one matters more than the spelling errors above.** Every other entry
in this issue is a slip of the keyboard. This one is evidence about *how* the
content is being produced: drafted by someone thinking in another language, or
run through a machine translator, and then published with no review pass. That
reframes the rest of this log — the run-together sentences (issue 37), the
missing words, the garbled word order, and the headings with no separator before
their second clause. They are not twenty unrelated typos. They are one
unreviewed pipeline.

The same page carries four more in its headings alone:

| Heading as published | What is wrong |
|---|---|
| Looking for "Fight Training Near Me"? **Sixth sense mma has covered** | The brand is lower case, and "you" is missing: it should read "has you covered" |
| Location Convenience Matters **of** Sixth Sense MMA | Word order reversed |
| Our Training Style **Discipline**, Strategy & Support — and How to Join SixthSense MMA **Simple** Steps | No separator, a colon or a dash, before the second clause |
| **Customers** Reviews: | Should be "Customer Reviews" |

**The same pattern again on `/bjj-classes-near-me/`, published hours later.** Two
of its eight H2s carry errors a proofreader would catch on the first pass:

| Heading as published | What is wrong |
|---|---|
| "What **is** BJJ classes **?**" | Subject-verb disagreement — "is" for "are" — and a space before the question mark, which is French typographic convention, not English |
| "Why Choose SixthSense MMA for **BJ** Classes Near You" | "BJ" for "BJJ". A letter is missing from the site's own primary keyword, in a heading, on a page about BJJ |

The **BJ** one is the more damaging of the two. It is an H2 on a page whose
entire purpose is to rank for "BJJ classes near me", so the keyword is
misspelt in one of the strongest on-page signals Google reads — and "BJ" carries
an unrelated meaning that a school marketing to children does not want beside
its brand name in a search result.

Five of its H3s also run two clauses together with no separator, the same habit
as the page before it: "BJJ vs. MMA **Which** One Fits You Better?", "Our Unique
Coaching Method **Focused**, Respectful, Real", "Transparent Pricing **No**
Hidden Fees", "Free First Class **Come** See for Yourself", and "Small Class
Sizes, Big Impact: One on One Attention".

**And again on `/martial-arts-gyms-near-me/`.** Its second H2 is "**What is**
martial arts**?**" — the same subject-verb disagreement as the BJJ page's "What
is BJJ classes ?", with "martial arts" in lower case in a heading where every
other noun is capitalised. Two of its H3s run their clauses together across a
no-break space — "Muay Thai⁠ The Art of Eight Limbs" and "Transparent Plans⁠ No
Hidden Charges" — and its H2 "Facility Tour What to Expect at SixthSense MMA"
does the same with an ordinary space.

The H1 itself has one: "Martial Arts Gyms Near **me** with Real Coaching and
Care" — title case for every word except "me". That is the page's single most
visible line of text and the one Google shows in the tab.

**A whole word missing from a heading, on `/mma-gyms-near-me/`.** Its fourth H2
reads:

> Why Choosing the Right MMA Gyms **Near** Isn't Just About Location

The word "Me" is absent after "Near". The heading is built on the page's own
primary keyword, "MMA gyms near me", and the keyword is incomplete in it.

The same post's H1 is "MMA Gyms Near **me** Giving **free** Trials and **Real
results**" — three words in the wrong case in one title, in two directions: "me"
and "free" lower case where the rest is title case, and "Real" capitalised while
"results" is not. Six of its headings run two clauses together with no separator
("Sixth Sense MMA Where Expertise Meets Real Community", "Trial Classes Are Free
Try Before You Commit", and four more), and one FAQ question is published as
"**hat** community aspects make SixthSense MMA stand out?" — the W is missing
from "What", so a question in the site's structured data begins mid-word.

Three of its nine testimonials also carry the same lack of a read-through: two
names are lower case ("**jansen** J., 27", "**danial** M., 32") and a third is
garbled across a tag boundary — "**kalin ssan** K., Jiu-Jitsu Competitor",
where "kalin" sits outside the bold tag that holds "ssan K.".

**A missing noun, and two words fused together, on `/mixed-martial-arts-near-me/`.**
Its fourth H2 reads:

> What Makes a Great **MMA** Near You?

A great MMA *what*? The noun — "gym" — is absent, so the heading asks an
incomplete question. This is the second heading in two days built on the site's
own keyword with a word missing from it, after "MMA Gyms **Near** Isn't Just
About Location" on `/mma-gyms-near-me/`.

Its second H2 writes the brand as "SixthSense **mma** has you covered" — the
suffix in lower case inside a heading that capitalises the rest of the name
correctly. And in the body:

> There are no hidden fees or confusing **contractsjust** honest coaching…

"contracts" and "just" are fused with no space and no punctuation between them —
in the sentence that makes the page's contractual claim.

The H1 continues the case problem: "Mixed Martial Arts **near me** with Proven
Real **results**" — two phrases in lower case in a title that capitalises
everything else, on the third consecutive post to do it.

**A fourth H1 with the same case problem, on `/brazilian-jiu-jitsu-near-me/`:**

> Brazilian Jiu Jitsu **near me Join now** with Flexible Pricing

Two phrases in lower case, and no punctuation at all between "me" and "Join", so
the title runs two sentences together. Four consecutive posts have now published
an H1 with the same fault.

Its H2 "How to Join a Brazilian Jiu Jitsu Class Near You **in** sixth sense mma"
uses "in" where English wants "at", and three of its H3s run clauses together
with no separator: "Personalized Training Plans **Not** One Size Fits All",
"Real Sparring, Real Growth **Safely**", "No Pressure, No Contracts **Just**
Progress".

**What to do:** this is the point to stop fixing individual typos and put a
proofreading step in front of publication. Whoever is drafting should have a
native English reader check each post before it goes live. Then correct these
headings, starting with "BJ Classes", the missing words, the missing "W" in the
FAQ question, "contractsjust", and the lower-case words in all four H1s.

### 33. The brand name written nine different ways, one of them in a page title — CONTENT, SEO — **High**

On `/muay-thai-training/` the business name is written **"sixth sense mma"** in
lowercase every time it appears — six times, and not once correctly. Every other
post on the site writes "Sixth Sense MMA".

| Where | Text as published |
|---|---|
| H2 | "Why **sixth sense mma** is the Right Place to Grow Through Muay Thai" |
| H2 | "Why Choose **sixth sense mma** for Your Muay Thai Journey" |
| H3 | "What You'll Experience at **sixth sense mma**:" |
| Body text | "At *sixth sense mma*, Muay Thai is more than…" |
| Body text | "…and at *sixth sense mma*, the focus is always…" |
| Image alt text | "Growth Through Self Discovery **sixth sense mma**" |

**Why it matters:** two of the six are section headings, which are among the
most visible text on the page and carry weight in search. A brand that writes
its own name inconsistently looks careless to a reader comparing gyms, and it
weakens the brand signal search engines build from repeated, consistent naming.

**A second post, with four spellings on one page.** `/muay-thai-muay-boran/`
manages to write the name four different ways:

| Form | Where |
|---|---|
| **sixthsensemma** (run together, lowercase) | H2: "Train Muay Thai Muay Boran the Right Way at sixthsensemma" |
| **sixth sense mma** (lowercase, spaced) | Alt text of the "Fist, Elbow, Knee, and Shin Use" image |
| **sixthsense mma** (half run together) | Alt text of the "Regional Styles" image |
| **Sixth Sense MMA** (correct) | Two headings and the body text |

So across these two posts the business name appears as "sixth sense mma",
"sixthsensemma", "sixthsense mma" and "Sixth Sense MMA", with the wrong forms in
section headings both times.

**A third post, mixing two spellings in three lines.** `/muay-thai-backpack/`
gets the name right in both of its headings — "Contact Sixth Sense MMA – We're
Here to Help" and "Why Choose Sixth Sense MMA?" — then writes it **"sixth sense
mma"**, in bold, in the first sentence under the second of them: "At **sixth
sense mma**, we care about quality, comfort, and your training needs." The
lowercase form is the emphasised text directly below a correctly-cased heading.

**`/10-muay-boran-techniques/` uses "SixthSense" seven times** and never writes
the name correctly, including in the H2 that closes the article ("Learn These
Techniques at **SixthSense** Real Training, Real Skills") and in a review it
quotes. No new variant, but it is now the third post in a row where the wrong
form is the only form.

**A sixth spelling, and a post that uses three of them at once.**
`/neck-bridges/` never writes the name correctly. It uses **"SixthSenseMMA"**
(five times), **"SixthSense"** (six more) and **"sixth sense mma"** (three,
including one in an H2 heading — "Training Plan & Progressions with trusted
parnter **sixth sense mma**"). Three variants on one page, none of them right.

That brings the site's total to six: **Sixth Sense MMA**, **sixth sense mma**,
**sixthsensemma**, **sixthsense mma**, **SixthSense** and **SixthSenseMMA**.

**A fifth spelling: "SixthSense", run together.** `/mma-fighting-styles/` uses
it **seven times** and never once writes the name correctly — not in the body,
not in the headings, not in the reviews it quotes:

| Where | Text as published |
|---|---|
| H2 | "Real MMA Training in Every Style at **SixthSense**" |
| H3 | "Why Choose **SixthSense** MMA Real Benefits We Offer" |
| Review | "**SixthSense** MMA made it possible." |
| Review | "Training at **SixthSense** changed my life." |
| Review | "**SixthSense** MMA taught him focus, discipline, and self control." |

So the site now writes its own name five ways: **Sixth Sense MMA**, **sixth
sense mma**, **sixthsensemma**, **sixthsense mma** and **SixthSense**. This post
is the first where the wrong form is the *only* form, which means a reader who
arrives here never sees the name spelled correctly.

**A seventh post, in a section heading, with the grammar broken too.**
`/are-there-multiple-styles-of-muay-boran/` heads its sales section "Why choose
**sixth sense mma** is trusted partner" — lowercase brand, and a sentence that
never resolves ("is trusted partner" for "is your trusted partner"). The
paragraph directly beneath it opens "**Sixth Sense MMA** isn't a typical gym",
correctly cased, so the two sit one line apart. The heading is the more visible
of the two.

**A sixth post, three times in body copy.** `/is-tai-chi-a-martial-art/` gets
the name right in its three section headings and wrong in the three sentences
underneath them: "At **sixth sense mma** , we strip away the myths", "At **sixth
sense mma** , we bridge tradition with…" and "Sites like **sixth sense mma** dive
deeper". Both of the first two also put a space before the comma. So the page
writes the brand correctly three times and incorrectly three times, and the
wrong version is always the one in the sentence a reader actually reads.

**A fifth post, in alt text twice.** `/muay-thai-thai-boxing/` writes the name
correctly nine times in its copy, then lowercase in both of its in-article image
descriptions: "Mental Toughness and Muay Thai: Strengthening Your Mind **sixth
sense mma**" and "Getting Started with Muay Thai Building Confidence from Day
One **sixth sense mma**". Alt text is exactly where these keep hiding — the same
slip as on `/muay-thai-muay-boran/`.

**A fourth post, where the brand is also the broken link.** On
`/is-wrestling-a-martial-art/` the Contact Us paragraph reads "Reach out to us
today via **sixth sense mma** to explore our classes" — lowercase, and
underlined with a `<u>` tag so it looks like a link. It is not one: the actual
`<a>` next to it wraps a single non-breaking space (see issue 40), so the
underlined brand name is unclickable and the real link is invisible. The post
gets the name right eight other times.

**A seventh form, on `/martial-arts-gyms-near-me/`.** The pricing section opens
"At **SixthSenseMMA**, we keep our pricing transparent" — the name run together
as one word, on a page that writes it "SixthSense MMA" fifteen other times. One
page, two spellings, three paragraphs apart.

**An eighth form, and three spellings in one page's headings, on
`/brazilian-jiu-jitsu-near-me/`.** This post manages three different versions of
the business name across its seven H2s alone:

| H2 | Brand as written |
|---|---|
| "Looking for Brazilian Jiu Jitsu Near Me? **Sixthsense mma** What to Expect" | **Sixthsense mma** — new form: one word, one capital, lower-case suffix |
| "How to Join a Brazilian Jiu Jitsu Class Near You in **sixth sense mma**" | **sixth sense mma** — all lower case |
| "Why **SixthSense MMA** Is the Right Place to Train BJJ" | **SixthSense MMA** — the correct form |

A reader scrolling this one page sees the gym's name spelt three ways in its
section headings, which are the largest text on the page after the title. The
body adds "SixthSense" on its own, so four variants in one article.

**POSITIVE, four in a row — the whole January 2026 batch is clean.**
`/muay-boran-techniques/` writes the brand as "**Sixth Sense MMA**" twice and
nothing else. With the three posts below it, that is **four consecutive January
2026 posts** with a single consistent spelling and not one run-together,
lower-cased or truncated variant between them.

Four for four settles it: the thirteen spellings catalogued below are all in
content published **before** January 2026. The newer process gets the brand right.
So the remedy is a one-off cleanup of the back catalogue, not a change to how
pages are being produced now.

Same caveat as the other three: the registered "Sixth Sense Martial Arts" appears
on none of the four, so the target form still needs deciding once, centrally.

**POSITIVE, three in a row, and this one is the longest page yet.**
`/mix-martial-arts-for-self-defense/` writes the brand as "**Sixth Sense MMA**"
**nineteen times and nothing else** — no run-together form, no bare "Sixth
Sense", no lower-case variant. Nineteen consistent instances across 4,242 words
is the strongest single result in this entry, and with the two posts below it
makes **three consecutive January 2026 posts** with one spelling.

The pattern is now clear enough to act on: everything published before January
2026 needs one find-and-replace per page, and nothing published since does.

Same caveat as the other two: the registered "Sixth Sense Martial Arts" appears
**nowhere** on any of the three, so the target form still needs deciding once,
centrally.

**POSITIVE, and now twice in a row.** `/martial-arts-weapons-training/` writes
the brand as "**Sixth Sense MMA**" **six times and nothing else** — no
run-together form, no bare "Sixth Sense", no lower-case variant. With
`/sambo-techniques-for-beginners/` below, that is **two consecutive posts** from
the January 2026 batch with a single consistent spelling.

Two clean pages in a row is the end of the "it cannot be helped" explanation. The
January posts are getting it right; everything before them needs one
find-and-replace each.

The same caveat applies: "Sixth Sense MMA" is the trading name and the registered
"Sixth Sense Martial Arts" appears **nowhere** on either page, so the target form
still needs deciding once, centrally.

**POSITIVE: the first page in the audit that writes the brand ONE way.**
`/sambo-techniques-for-beginners/` uses "**Sixth Sense MMA**" **eleven times and
nothing else** — no run-together "SixthSense MMA", no bare "Sixth Sense", no
lower-case variant, no hashtag. Across 95 posts and thirteen recorded spellings,
this is the first page with a single consistent form from the H1 to the
conclusion.

It is worth saying because it rules out the remaining explanations for the other
pages. Whoever produced this one managed it, so the variation elsewhere is not a
tooling limitation or a house-style gap — it is proofreading. One find-and-replace
per page, with this page as the reference, closes the whole issue.

The only caveat: "Sixth Sense MMA" is the trading name, not the registered "Sixth
Sense Martial Arts", which appears **nowhere** on this page. That is the same gap
as `/best-martial-arts-for-self/` below, so the target form still needs deciding
once, centrally.

**A second page where the business's real name never appears at all.**
`/best-martial-arts-for-self/` writes the brand three ways and **"Sixth Sense
Martial Arts" is not one of them**:

| Form | Times | Where |
|---|---|---|
| **SixthSense MMA** | 5 | the **H1**, the body, and the page's own `<title>` |
| Sixth Sense MMA | 2 | an H2 and the Follow Us paragraph |
| **Sixth Sense** (bare) | 1 | an H2: "Why Choose **Sixth Sense** for Best Martial Arts for Self Defense" |

The run-together form outnumbers the correct one five to two and holds the H1, as
on the weapons page below. The bare "Sixth Sense" in an H2 trails off without
"Martial Arts" or "MMA" after it, the same fault as the Muay Boran page's
lower-cased "by sixth sense".

**And the run-together form wins on the weapons page, in the H1 again.**
`/martial-arts-weapons-training-in-coppell/` uses only two spellings, but the
wrong one is in the H1 and in two H2s: "**SixthSense MMA**" appears **6 times**,
including "Martial Arts Weapons Training in Coppell TX Safe Techniques at
SixthSense MMA" (the H1), "Why Choose SixthSense MMA for Martial Arts Weapons in
Coppell TX" and the page's own `<title>`. "Sixth Sense MMA" appears **9 times** in
the body. The correct full name, "Sixth Sense Martial Arts", appears **not once** —
the first page on the site where the business's actual name is entirely absent.

**The worst single page yet: EIGHT spellings of the brand in 2,242 words, plus a
hashtag and a truncated form.** `/muay-boran-in-coppell/` writes the business
name eleven different ways:

| Form | Times | Where |
|---|---|---|
| Sixth Sense Martial Arts | 5 | body |
| sixth sense martial arts | 1 | body, lower case |
| **sixth sense martial** (no "arts") | 2 | "Class at sixth sense martial fits adults and youth" |
| Sixth Sense MMA | 1 | body |
| Sixth sense mma | 2 | body |
| sixth sense mma | 4 | body, including "At choose sixth sense mma" |
| **SixthSense MMA** | 3 | **the H1, an H3 and the Contact section** |
| **Sixthsensemma** | 1 | "Sixthsensemma focuses on real feel from ancient martial art." |
| **sixth sense** | 1 | **an H3: "Who Should Learn Muay Boran in Coppell by sixth sense"** |
| #sixthsensemartialarts | 1 | dropped into a body paragraph as a hashtag |

Three of these are new faults rather than new spellings. **"sixth sense
martial"** is the name cut off mid-phrase, twice, and reads as a typo. **"by
sixth sense"** is an H3 ending in the brand in lower case with nothing after it,
so the heading trails off. And **"#sixthsensemartialarts"** is an Instagram
hashtag published inside article prose — "#sixthsensemartialarts calls those
ready for real work" — which belongs in a social caption, not a web page.

The correct "Sixth Sense Martial Arts" is the most frequent form at 5, but the
**H1 uses "SixthSense MMA"**, so the first and largest instance of the name on
the page is wrong, exactly as on `/bjj-belt-ranks/` below.

**A thirteenth spelling: "SixthSense MMA", run together but spaced before MMA.**
`/bjj-belt-ranks/` writes the brand as **"SixthSense MMA"** — no space between
"Sixth" and "Sense", but one before "MMA" — **eleven times** in its body, including
in its H1 and in two H2s ("Benefits of BJJ Belt Ranks at SixthSense MMA", "Why
Choose SixthSense MMA for BJJ Belt Ranks"). The correct "Sixth Sense MMA" appears
**once**, and the page's own meta description uses the run-together form too.

This is distinct from the eight forms already listed: it is not "SixthSenseMMA"
(all run together, logged below) and not "SixthSense" alone. Eleven to one, with the
wrong form in the headings, is the same ratio problem as `/types-of-sambo/`.

**The worst case so far: one page writes the run-together form 31 times.**
`/types-of-sambo/` uses **SixthSenseMMA** — no spaces, three internal capitals —
**31 times** in its body copy, and the correct "Sixth Sense MMA" **5 times** on
the same page. Nearly every paragraph on the post opens with it:
