high in the page, inside "Local, Safe & Clean Environment", so the sideways
scroll it causes on a phone is the first thing a visitor meets. Only the embed's coordinates differ, and
on `/fight-training-near-me/` the map sits inside the "Location Convenience"
section, which is the part of the page most likely to be read on a phone while
someone is standing outside.

**What to do:** give all eight iframes `width="100%"`, a `title`, and `loading="lazy"`,
copying the pattern already used on the program pages.

**In the rebuild:** the embed is reproduced exactly as published, inline style,
fixed width and all, because the brief is a faithful replica. It is the only
inline style anywhere in the rebuilt site.

### 34. WordPress editor markup published in a page heading — SEO, CONTENT — Medium

On `/muay-thai-weight-classes/` the H1 is published with the block editor's own
attributes still attached:

```
<h1 class="wp-block wp-block-post-title block-editor-block-list__block
    editor-post-title editor-post-title__input rich-text"
    role="textbox" contenteditable="true" aria-label="Add title"
    aria-multiline="true">
```

`contenteditable="true"` means **a visitor can click the headline and type over
it** in their own browser. Nothing is saved, but it looks broken, and anyone can
screenshot the page with a headline of their choosing. `role="textbox"` and
`aria-label="Add title"` are worse for screen reader users: the page's main
heading is announced as an empty edit field labelled "Add title", not as the
title of the article.

This is editor chrome that escaped into the published page, most likely by
pasting markup copied out of the editor rather than typing the title into the
title field.

**Affected:** `/muay-thai-weight-classes/`, the H1.

**What to do:** retype the title in the editor's title field so WordPress
outputs a plain `<h1>`. Check any other post where the title was pasted in, and
search the database for `contenteditable` to catch the rest.

**In the rebuild:** the page uses the site's own clean `<h1>`, and the build
fails if any editor attribute survives.

### 35. Weight chart figures that disagree with each other — CONTENT — Medium, to verify

`/muay-thai-weight-classes/` publishes four tables of weight limits, which is
exactly the kind of content readers act on. Three things in them do not line up,
and none was changed in the rebuild:

- **The top division is named twice, differently.** The main chart's heaviest
  entry is "Heavyweight, ~95.3+ kg / ~210+ lbs", but the speed-versus-power
  table calls the same bracket "Super Heavyweight (210+ lbs)".
- **Every figure in the main chart is approximate** ("~47.6 kg", "~105 lbs").
  Competition limits are exact numbers a fighter has to make, so a reader
  checking whether they fit a division cannot rely on a tilde.
- **The stadium comparison gives Lumpinee and Rajadamnern the same 105–160 lbs
  range**, which is narrower than the divisions those stadiums actually run, and
  it stops well below the chart's own heavier classes. Worth checking against
  each organisation's published limits.

**What to do:** have someone who competes check the tables against the current
Lumpinee, Rajadamnern and ONE Championship limits, replace the approximations
with exact figures, and use one name per division throughout. A "last checked"
date next to the chart would help, since these limits change.

**In the rebuild:** every number, unit and tilde is copied exactly as published.
Nothing was converted, rounded or corrected.

### 31. Legal, street-fighting and injury-risk content published with no disclaimer — CONTENT — **High, liability**

**A page that teaches elbow strikes to the temple and arm dislocations — and then
offers recorded drills for HOME practice.**
`/muay-boran-techniques/` is the first page in the audit to pair high-risk
technique content with an explicit invitation to practise it unsupervised.

**What it teaches.** The "Specific Top 10 Muay Boran Techniques" table names an
anatomical target in almost every row, reproduced verbatim:

| # | Name | Description, as published |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Hanuman Thawai Waen | "Spinning punch and piercing dagger strike **to face and throat**." |
| 2 | Phra Ram Yeab Longa | "Double uppercuts and snapping strike **to skull or chin**." |
| 3 | Crocodile Tail Kick | "Heel kick to thigh or leg **causing dislocation**." |
| 6 | Nao Taeng Krit | "Shoulder and elbow attack **targeting temple and neck**." |
| 9 | Hong Peek Hak | "**Arm dislocation technique** for close combat dominance." |

And in the prose: "Start with a jab to close distance follow with **powerful elbow
strike to the temple** then drop a knee strikes to the body", "muay boran elbow
strikes **to ribs** break holds", "**Grapple breaks use elbow spikes to joints**".
The temple is one of the most dangerous targets on the head.

**What the page does well.** Its supervision copy is genuinely good: a **kru** is
named seven times ("Kru watch closely to correct posture prevent bad habits and
**stop drills if needed**", "Kru supervise closely encourage questions and promote
tapping early"), breakfalls are taught ("Falls get taught with proper breakfalls
to avoid injury"), and the gear is specific — "padded mats", "hand wraps shin
guards", "wraps gloves and shin guards", "light partners", "no full power until
form locks in".

**And then it undoes it.** Under the H3 "Group Classes One on One Coaching and
Online Options":

> "Online options include video lessons live virtual sessions and **recorded muay
> boran training drills for home practice**."

> "**Online access** adds extra value for travel or busy days."

So the same page that insists a kru must watch every drill also sells recordings
to work through at home — of elbow strikes to the temple, arm dislocations and
throws. No other page in this entry does that. Everything the supervision
paragraphs establish is optional the moment a reader chooses the online track.

**No disclaimer and no legal reference.** "Disclaimer", "at your own risk", "seek
medical", "legal", "law" and "lawful" appear **nowhere** on the page.

**What to do:** either drop the home-practice offer, or restrict the recorded
material to what is safe alone — stances, footwork, shadow work, conditioning —
and say so in the sentence. The page already distinguishes those: "Light contact
pad work and **shadow practice** build confidence before partner drills."

**Eye gouges, groin strikes, head butts and "using one person as a shield" — with
no legal reference anywhere on the page.**
`/mix-martial-arts-for-self-defense/` is the most explicit page on the site about
injuring a person, and it is the only one of the violence pages that makes **no
legal claim at all**. Both halves of that matter.

**a) What it teaches.** Reproduced verbatim:

> "**Self defense fighting techniques include eye gouges groin strikes and head
> butts when rules disappear.**"

> "**Multiple attackers get addressed through fast positioning and using one
> person as a shield.**"

> "Striking arts in sport avoid dirty targets while real world self defense **hits
> vulnerable spots** to neutralize threats."

> "Jab cross hook and uppercut drills sharpen punch placement so **every strike
> lands on weak spots like chin nose or body**."

> "Elbows slice across faces or cut through guards with short sharp arcs that
> **open cuts and stun fast**." · "Knees drive upward into **midsection thighs or
> head**."

> "Krav maga adds instinctive defenses against common grabs chokes or ground pins
> with **eye strikes groin kicks** and fast breaks."

> "Joint locks target arms shoulders wrists or legs with slow pressure that forces
> taps or **breaks resistance**." · "body locks trips and **body slams** control
> the fight flow" · "**dumping someone hard** then standing back up to face
> others" · "**forcing the opponent against a wall** for advantage".

That is further than `/kidney-punch/` goes on named targets, and the page names
no body part it tells a reader to avoid.

**b) Legal claims: NONE, and that is the finding.** The words "**legal**",
"**law**", "**lawful**", "**justified**" and "**proportion**" do not appear
anywhere on the page. So a 4,242-word article that teaches eye gouges and head
butts as self-defence contains **no reference to the circumstances in which force
is permitted**. Texas law distinguishes reasonable from excessive force and
treats a deadly-force response differently from a proportionate one; "using one
person as a shield" and "eye gouges" are at the far end of that scale. The other
four pages in this entry at least make a legal claim that can be reviewed — this
one leaves the subject out entirely, which is harder to spot and no safer.

**c) A disclaimer: none.** "Disclaimer", "at your own risk", "seek medical",
"consult a doctor" and "not responsible" appear nowhere.

**And the outcome claims are the strongest in the cluster**, though none of them
is an outright guarantee — the word "guarantee" never appears:

> "Self defense martial training in wrestling builds **unbreakable** base" ·
> "Wrestling teaches **unbreakable** balance" · "builds confidence since **you
> know escapes and finishes work** even when tired or surprised" · "Martial arts
> self protection through wrestling **works against untrained attacker**" ·
> "**Everyone walks away stronger safer** and more confident".

**What to do, in order:** add the legal frame — one paragraph on when force is
justified, written by someone qualified; add the disclaimer; and move the eye
gouges, groin strikes and head butts sentence behind that frame or cut it. **In
the rebuild** every sentence above is reproduced exactly and nothing is
corrected.

**A disarm claim, a live-blade gap and a promise to teach weapons law — on a page
with no disclaimer and no minimum age.**
`/martial-arts-weapons-training/` was read for exactly these four things and the
findings are specific.

**a) Does it describe how to strike, disarm or injure with a weapon?** It
**claims** disarm instruction and teaches no mechanics. The strongest sentence is:

> "**Disarming techniques teach how to take away stick nunchaku bo staff
> longsword or knife from an attacker.**"

Also: "Students practice safe ways to **wield a weapon and gain control in close
range**", "Classes cover **responses to knife stick sword blade** and other melee
threats", "Students practice linking **strike spin block and flail movements with
nunchaku longsword blade and stick**", "Students train to read opponents… and
**land precise hits at the right moment**".

**Every one of those is a description of what a class covers, not an
instruction.** There is no angle, no body target, no force, no grip detail and no
edge alignment anywhere on the page — the words "target", "artery", "temple",
"pressure point" and "full force" do not appear. So it is **not** in the same
category as `/kidney-punch/`, which gives step-by-step mechanics. It advertises
dangerous instruction without providing any.

**b) Claims about effectiveness in a real confrontation.** Four, and all four use
the word "any" with no hedge:

> "so you feel confident and **ready for any self defense situation or combat**" ·
> "so students **feel ready for any situation**" · "The goal is to build
> confidence so students **feel ready for any combat threat**" · "Timing distance
> accuracy control and reflex **prepare you for any threat** making overall
> martial arts skills **strong and reliable**"

**c) Does it distinguish training weapons from live blades?** **Partly, and the
gap is the problem.** It names practice weapons three times — the H3 "Traditional
Practice Weapons (**Wood & Foam**)", "use only high quality professional equipment
including traditional practice weapons made from **wood and foam** for nunchaku bo
sword stick and blade work", and "New students first practice… with **simple
training weapons**". But it **never says a live blade is excluded**, and "knife",
"sword", "longsword" and "blade" appear throughout as things students handle:
"Students learn to hold stick bo staff nunchaku sword and **knife**", "blade
handling helps develop spin wield control". A reader cannot tell from the page
whether the knife in "hold… sword and knife" is wood or steel.

**d) A safety disclaimer?** **No.** "Disclaimer", "at your own risk", "seek
medical", "consult a doctor" and "not responsible" appear nowhere.

**e) A minimum age?** **No** — see issue 39. "No matter your age or background."

**LEGAL, for client review.** The page makes exactly one legal reference, and it
is different in kind from the Coppell page's:

> "Students also explore improvised weapons and **basic martial arts weapons laws
> usa** to stay responsible while building practical skills in weapons techniques
> empty hand training and unarmed combat."

This is a claim to **teach the law**, not a statement of what the law is. It
names no jurisdiction, no statute and no age restriction, and it is vaguer than
the Coppell page's "fit urban needs and legal carry" — which makes it harder to
disprove and harder to stand behind. It is now the **fourth** page carrying an
unreviewed legal claim, after `/muay-thai-vs-kickboxing/`,
`/martial-blade-concepts/` and
`/martial-arts-weapons-training-in-coppell/`. **In the rebuild** it is reproduced
exactly and nothing is corrected.

**What to do:** state in one line which weapons are wood or foam and that live
blades are not handled; add the disclaimer; add a minimum age; and either drop
the weapons-law claim or have someone qualified write that section.

**HEEL HOOKS taught to beginners — and, on the same page, the supervision copy
the other four should be rewritten toward.**
`/sambo-techniques-for-beginners/` is written for people who have never trained.
Under the H3 "Beginner Level Submission Awareness" it publishes:

> "Sambo leg locks start with simple ankle pressure or **heel hooks** in
> controlled drills."

> "**Joint locks on elbows or ankle cranks** appear as options after good
> control."

**The heel hook is the single highest-risk submission in grappling.** The knee
gives way before the pain registers, so the usual protection — tap before it
hurts — does not work. It is banned below the highest competition belts in IBJJF
rules and excluded from beginner curricula in most gyms. A page that lists it
among the things a first-week student meets, alongside "simple ankle pressure",
understates it by placing it in that company. **Flag at HIGH.**

**Now the other half, because it changes what to do about it.** This page is
**not** in the same position as `/neck-bridges/`,
`/do-neck-bridges-build-muscle/` and `/kidney-punch/`. Those three teach a
movement or a strike with no mention of a coach. This one is the most
supervision-conscious page in the entire audit:

| | `/sambo-techniques-for-beginners/` |
|---|---|
| a) Does it tell beginners to practise throws or locks without an instructor? | **No.** Every drill is described in a class. Its only solo work is explicitly stage one of a coached progression: "progress slowly from **solo shadow work** to light partner practice then controlled live rolling" |
| b) Breakfalls, mats, a training partner? | **All three, repeatedly.** "proper falling techniques called **ukemi** right away" · "stance footwork grip fighting and **ukemi falls**" · "Classes use **mats with good cushioning**" · "**Mats provide full cushioning** and space for safe falls" · "**Pairs** practice controlled exchanges where **partners** help each other" · "**Partners agree on speed and power before starting**" · "partner cooperates on the fall" — a coach is named **13 times**, a partner **15 times** |
| c) A safety disclaimer? | **No.** "Disclaimer", "at your own risk", "seek medical", "consult a doctor" and "not responsible" appear nowhere |

**So the recommendation is the reverse of the usual one.** Do not rewrite this
page's safety approach — **copy it onto the other four**. "Coach watches closely
during every drill and stops action if form slips", "Tapping out happens early
and often during holds to stop pressure before it hurts", "Partners agree on
speed and power before starting" and "Coach supervision stays constant with eyes
on every pair" are the sentences `/neck-bridges/` and `/kidney-punch/` are
missing, already written in the client's own voice.

**Two things still to fix here:** move heel hooks out of the beginner section,
and add the disclaimer this page, like the other four, does not have.

**Outcome promises on a self-defence page — and the only no-guarantee caveat on
the site, on the same page.** `/best-martial-arts-for-self/` makes these claims,
all reproduced verbatim and none altered:

> "**Gains in calm strength and awareness last forever.**" — a permanence claim.
> Nothing from training lasts forever without training.

> "**Technique over strength makes small beat big here.**" — stated as fact, with
> no qualifier, on a page aimed at "Men and Women Seeking Personal Safety".

> "**Calm grows from knowing you can protect.**" · "**Personal safety rises** with
> drills against grabs or strikes." · "**Personal safety becomes habit** from
> repeated drills." · "Young adults build **life long** self-defense skills early."

None of these guarantees a reader can defend themselves in so many words, and
that distinction is worth keeping: the page never says "you will be able to
defend yourself". But "makes small beat big" and "last forever" are both stated
without hedge, to an audience being told elsewhere on the page that the training
is for "real protection skills".

**And then the page does the thing none of the others do.** In its first section:

> "**No martial art guarantees success but good ones build fighting ability
> through practice.**"

That is the only explicit refusal of an outcome guarantee in the whole content
library, and it is the nearest thing to a disclaimer on any of the pages in this
entry. It is also the fix: the client's own wording, in the client's own voice,
already exists and can be lifted onto the other pages.

**What is still missing:** no disclaimer in the formal sense — "disclaimer", "at
your own risk", "seek medical", "consult a doctor" and "not responsible" appear
nowhere — and one absence-of-injury sentence had to be cut (issue 0).

**A leg-lock page with no disclaimer, and the word "supervision" never appears.**
`/sambo-techniques-in-coppell/` devotes two H3s to leg locks — "Leg Locks as a
Signature of Sambo" and "Ankle Locks and Knee Locks" — and describes them
mechanically: "**Ankle locks twist heels inward for straight foot pain while knee
locks bar or compress joints**", "Sambo classics include **figure four and toe
holds** for tight control", "Sambo training stresses **grip and hip push for
power**". Leg locks are the submissions most likely to injure an untrained
partner, because the joint gives way before the pain does.

**What the page does well, and it is worth saying:** its submission section is
the best safety copy on any of these pages — "Safety comes first with **taps and
coach watch** during submission drills at our gym", "Control means **slow squeeze
and clear signals** to avoid injury", "Sambo training teaches **when to release
for partner health**". Nothing on the page invites a reader to try anything alone.

**What is missing:**

- **No disclaimer of any kind.** "Disclaimer", "at your own risk", "seek
  medical", "consult a doctor" and "not responsible" appear nowhere — the same
  gap as the four pages below.
- **The word "supervision" never appears**, only "coach watch", and only for
  submissions.
- **The throwing sections have no supervision statement at all**, while
  encouraging daily repetition: "Sambo fighters **drill hips daily**",
  "**Practicing sambo daily** builds the needed reps", "Sambo trening means
  practice these often". Hip throws, shoulder throws and lifts — "**Lifting
  techniques raise foes off the ground** ... to set up slams" — get no equivalent
  of the submission section's caution.
- **No minimum age**, on a page about leg locks (issue 39).

**In the rebuild** nothing was added: a disclaimer would be new copy and its
wording is the client's decision. **What to do:** copy the submission section's
own sentence into the throwing sections, and add the standard disclaimer.

**A weapons page making a legality claim, with no jurisdiction and no
disclaimer — for client legal review.**
`/martial-arts-weapons-training-in-coppell/` publishes these sentences verbatim.
They are reproduced unchanged in the rebuild and **nothing has been corrected**:

> "These martial arts training weapon options fit urban needs and **legal carry**."

> "**Tactical tools** include collapsible batons or flashlights designed for self
> defense."

> "**Kubotans** are small keychain sticks for pressure point strikes in close
> range."

> Of the hanbo: "**It hides easily** and works for leverage against larger foes."
> · "Training emphasizes **concealment and quick draw**." · "This unique martial
> arts weapons tool boosts **everyday carry options**."

> Of the dan bong: "**It attaches to keychains for discreet self defense**."

> "These weapons in karate come from times when common folk needed **hidden
> defenses**."

**Why this needs a lawyer, not an editor.** "Fit urban needs and legal carry" is
a legal conclusion stated as fact, with **no state, no statute and no age
limit** named anywhere on the page. Texas law on clubs, batons and bladed weapons
is specific: it changed in 2019, it treats location (schools, bars, places of
worship) differently from general carry, and it restricts people under 18
separately. A page that tells readers these tools "fit legal carry" and then
teaches **concealment and quick draw** is making a claim the gym may not be able
to stand behind, on a page that also invites children (issue 39) and names no
minimum age.

The page is also the **third** on the site to publish an unreviewed legal claim,
after `/muay-thai-vs-kickboxing/` and `/martial-blade-concepts/`. Three pages,
three different legal assertions, no review on any of them.

**And no disclaimer, again.** The words "disclaimer", "at your own risk", "seek
medical", "consult a doctor" and "not responsible" appear **nowhere** on a page
about katana, sickles, chain whips and a dagger "**often poisoned historically
for lethality**". **In the rebuild** no disclaimer was invented, because writing
one would be writing new copy and because the wording is a decision for the
client's lawyer.

**A page about organ trauma with no disclaimer either.**
`/are-kidney-shots-legal-in-ufc/` devotes a whole H2 to what a kidney strike does
to the body — "the shock travels straight into the internal organ", "an instant
wave of nausea burning pain that spreads through the lower back", "it starts a slow
torture that gets worse every minute", "internal bleeding laceration contusion and
possible organ failure" — and carries **no disclaimer of any kind**. The words
"disclaimer", "consult a doctor", "physician", "medical professional", "not medical
advice", "seek medical advice" and "do not attempt" appear **zero times**.

That makes **four pages** in this batch describing injury mechanisms with no
disclaimer: `/neck-bridges/`, `/do-neck-bridges-build-muscle/`, `/sambo-moves/`
and this one, alongside `/kidney-punch/` already logged at issue 47. One policy,
applied once, fixes all five.

**A SECOND neck bridge post, also with no disclaimer.**
`/do-neck-bridges-build-muscle/` (16 November 2025) is the second article on this
exercise and, like `/neck-bridges/`, it carries **no medical disclaimer of any
kind**. The words "disclaimer", "consult a doctor", "consult your physician",
"medical professional", "not medical advice" and "seek medical advice" appear
**zero times** in 1,804 words.

What it does publish is a full prescription:

| Where | Published instruction |
|---|---|
| Recommended reps &amp; sets | "Beginner 3 sets 10 to 20 seconds static holds." |
| | "Progressively increase 5 seconds weekly." |
| | "Neck workouts 3 4 sets advanced." · "Combat sports 4 sets." |
| | "Neck harness work 8 12 reps." |
| Step by step | "Lie on back. Plant feet. Lift hips. Place head on mat. Rock gently." |
| Alignment | "Plate on your forehead only advanced." |
| Frequency (table) | "Can be trained daily (light)" |
| FAQ | "Beginners should hold bridges for 10–20 seconds" · "extended to 30–60 seconds" |

That is a dated, numbered loading programme for the cervical spine, published to
beginners, including an instruction to put **a weight plate on the forehead**, on
a site whose programmes start at age five.

**It is better than `/neck-bridges/` in one respect and that matters:** it says
"Neck bridges aren't for all", it names herniation risk, and it repeats
**"Medical clearance if neck issues" four times**. A clearance prompt is not a
disclaimer — it is one line of fragment copy inside a comparison table and a body
paragraph, with no explanation of what a neck issue is or who to ask — but it is
more than the first post managed, and the right fix is to build on it rather than
start from nothing.

**Priority: HIGH, alongside the existing `/neck-bridges/` and `/kidney-punch/`
flags.** Two posts now prescribe cervical-spine loading with no disclaimer, and
`/sambo-moves/` prescribes joint locks with none either. That is three pages, and
the pattern is the absence of a site-wide policy rather than three separate
oversights.

**What to do:** write one disclaimer and put it on every page that prescribes a
movement — the two neck bridge posts, `/kidney-punch/`, `/sambo-moves/`,
`/sambo-techniques/` and `/mae-mai-muay-thai-what-is-it/`. It should name a
clinician, not just "clearance", and it should say explicitly that the article is
not individual medical advice. **The rebuild adds none** — inventing a disclaimer
the client has not approved would be writing new copy into a replica — so the
rebuilt page carries the same absence and the build fails if one is introduced
without being asked for.

**A second Sambo post does the same, and this one addresses five-year-olds.**
`/sambo-moves/` is subtitled "Essential Techniques for **Beginners**" in its page
title and carries joint-lock, throw and takedown instruction with **no supervision
caveat anywhere on the page** — the words "supervision", "qualified", "instructor
present", "do not attempt" and "seek professional" appear zero times in 2,907
words.

What it does publish:

> **Locking Moves** — "Joint locks trap elbows. Leg locks snap ankles. Locking
> moves crank slow. Submission techniques end pain."

> **Arm Lock** — "Arm lock cranks elbows. **Joint locks hyperextend fast.**
> Submission techniques force taps from mount."

> **Leg Hook** — "Leg hook reaps thigh. Sambo moves inside or outside. Throwing
> techniques lift knee. Sambo fighters drive hip through."

> **Strong Takedowns** — "Strong takedowns crash mats. Hip throw flips hips.
> Double legs blast low."

"Leg locks snap ankles" and "Joint locks hyperextend fast" describe the mechanism
of injury as the goal of the technique. Heel hooks and ankle locks are banned for
juveniles and for beginners by most grappling federations precisely because the
injury arrives without warning.

**The page then applies all of it to children.** An H3 reads "Perfect Martial Arts
Training for **Kids 5+**", and under it: "Kids roll safe from age five. [...]
Throws and takedowns play light." Another says "Kids start age five with throws.
**Adults chase leg locks.**" A five-year-old's parent reading this page is told
throws are appropriate from age five, on a page that has just explained how to
hyperextend an elbow.

**Priority: HIGH, alongside `/neck-bridges/` and `/kidney-punch/`.** Those two are
already logged here for the same fault — instruction in a technique that injures,
published to an audience the page itself identifies as untrained, with no caveat.
This is the third, and the only one of the three that names an age as low as five.

**What this is NOT:** the page does not explicitly tell a reader to practise alone
or at home. There is no "try this at home" sentence. The fault is the absence of
the opposite: a page titled for beginners, naming ages from five, giving
joint-lock mechanics, and saying nothing at all about needing a coach.

**What to do:** add a supervision line to every technique section on all four Sambo
posts, `/neck-bridges/` and `/kidney-punch/`, and remove the leg-lock references
from anything addressed to children. **The rebuild does not add one** — inventing a
disclaimer the client has not approved would be writing new copy into a replica —
so `dist/sambo-moves.html` carries the same absence and the build fails if a
supervision note is ever introduced without being asked for.

**Joint-lock and leg-lock instruction on a page whose title invites beginners to
start today.** `/sambo-techniques/` is titled "Learn sambo techniques that
**beginners can start today**" and carries step-by-step instruction for the two
categories of technique most likely to injure an untrained partner:

> ### Arm Locks and Joint Manipulations
> Start by securing your opponent's arm often after a throw or takedown. Twist
> the arm while pinning their body to limit movement. **Common moves like the
> armbar hyperextend the elbow** making taps quick in sambo competitions. Joint
> manipulations also include **wrist locks**…

> ### Leg Locks for Control
> **Target the knee or ankle with moves like the heel hook or kneebar.** After a
> takedown isolate their leg by trapping it between yours. **Twist or extend to
> apply pressure** forcing a submission or limiting their movement.

The **heel hook** is the specific concern. It is widely restricted in grappling
competition for beginners and banned outright in many rulesets precisely because
it loads the knee in a way the person being held often cannot feel until damage
is done. The page names it in a list of moves to "target", to a reader it has
told can start today.

**Three sacrifice-throw and hip-throw sequences** carry the same shape — "Roll
backward pulling them over you", "Your hip acts as the fulcrum push it into their
lower body" — which are falls that need a coach and a mat.

**To the page's credit, it carries one line of its own:**

> Sambo fighters train these carefully as they **can cause injury if mishandled**.

That is the only safety wording on 5,271 words, it sits inside the leg-lock
section only, and it describes the risk rather than telling the reader what to do
about it.

**This is the third instance in this issue**, after the neck-bridge progressions
and the kidney-punch article. The pattern is the same each time: genuinely
competent technical writing, published with no supervision note, on a site whose
audience includes parents of children.

**What to do:** add one sentence at the top of the technique sections — "These
techniques need a qualified coach and a mat. Do not attempt joint locks or leg
locks on a partner without supervision." It costs nothing, it does not weaken the
article, and it is the difference between instructional content and an
instruction. The same sentence serves the neck-bridge and kidney-punch pages.

**In the rebuild:** every technique description is reproduced word for word and
**no supervision note has been added** — the build fails if one appears, because
inventing safety copy the client has not approved would misrepresent the live
page. The recommendation is the client's to action.



`/muay-thai-vs-kickboxing/` has a section headed "Legal Considerations in
Self-Defense Scenarios" that makes statements about what the law allows. It
says laws "typically emphasize the use of **reasonable force** and the
importance of de-escalating a situation whenever possible", that "a point in
legal defense is to avoid ground fighting", and that readers should
"familiarize yourself with local laws before applying any martial technique".

**Why it is worth a look:**
- Self-defence law differs by state, and the page names no state or country,
  while the academy serves Coppell, Texas.
- It reads as guidance on what a reader may lawfully do in a violent
  encounter, published by a business that teaches them to fight. A reader could
  act on it.
- There is no disclaimer anywhere on the page.

**What to do:** this is a judgement call for the client, not a factual error to
correct, so nothing was changed. The usual fix is a short line at the top or
foot of that section saying the information is general, is not legal advice,
and that laws vary by state — and, if they want to keep the specifics, having
someone qualified read the section first. The same check is worth applying to
any other post that touches on law.

**A fifth post, about children training unsupervised.**
`/mae-mai-muay-thai-what-is-it/` has an H2 headed "**Can kids learn these at
home?**", and its FAQ opens with "Can I train Mae Mai at home?". Both are
answered encouragingly, on a page that has just listed fifteen striking and
clinching techniques including elbow strikes to the neck, knee strikes to the
ribs and arm-twisting takedowns.

This is a much milder case than the neck bridges or kidney punch pages — Mae Mai
practice is ordinary Muay Thai training, and nothing on the page tells a child to
do anything dangerous. But it is a section specifically about **children
practising strikes at home, away from a coach**, published by the gym that would
otherwise be supervising them, with no line anywhere saying a parent should be
present or that these are best learned in class.

**What to do:** add one sentence to that section — children should practise with
an adult present and learn the techniques in class first. It costs nothing, it is
what the gym would say in person, and it also happens to be an argument for
booking a class rather than against it.

**A sixth post, and the first to advise on street fights directly:
`/mma-vs-kickboxing-for-self-defense/`.** The site's longest article, 6,785
words, carries two sections written about real violence outside a gym:

**"Limitations of Each in Street Fights"** is a 29-row table comparing what
kickboxing and MMA cannot do in an unsanctioned fight — "No ground defense when
down", "Limited to stand-up only", "Weak in close quarters", and so on. It is a
tactical assessment of how each art fails in a street fight.

**"Protective Attire for Self-Defense Scenarios"** goes further and advises on
what to wear and use:

> In self-defense situations, protective attire is minimal, often consisting of
> street clothes… **Sturdy shoes can be helpful**, but gloves and headgear are
> not typically practical. **The goal is to adapt to the situation and use
> what's available.**

"Use what's available" in a violent encounter is advice about improvised
weapons, however lightly it is phrased. Neither section carries a disclaimer,
and neither mentions de-escalation, leaving, or calling the police — the three
things every responsible self-defence curriculum puts first.

**Why this one belongs here rather than being waved through as comparison
content.** The page is not describing how a sport works; it is telling a reader
how to conduct themselves in a street fight, and it is doing so on a site whose
five program pages include **Kids BJJ, Teen BJJ and Teen Muay Thai**. A
thirteen-year-old reading "adapt to the situation and use what's available" is
being given a different message from the one the gym gives in class.

It also sits three clicks from `/kidney-punch/` (issue 47) and `/neck-bridges/`,
so the site now has a small cluster of pages about hurting people or getting
hurt, none of them disclaimed.

**What to do:** add the same short disclaimer used for the other five, and add
one sentence to "Protective Attire" saying the first goal in any confrontation
is to leave safely. If the client would rather not carry street-fight content at
all on a site marketing to children, the two sections can be cut without
weakening the comparison, which stands on its own as a sports article.

**A fourth post, and the sharpest case: `/kidney-punch/`.** It describes damage
to an internal organ — kidney trauma, internal bleeding, the reasons the strike
is banned — and carries no disclaimer of any kind. It is covered in full under
issue 47, which deals with the instructional problem; it belongs here too
because the disclaimer gap is identical to the neck bridges page: an article
that describes serious physical harm in detail, published by the business that
would supervise the training, with nothing telling the reader to see a doctor if
it happens to them. The page's own FAQ asks "What should I do if I get punched
in the kidney in training?" and answers it without once saying "seek medical
attention" in any formal way.

**Raised to High, and it is no longer only about the law.** This entry began as
two posts making claims about self-defence and knife law. `/neck-bridges/` adds
a different kind of exposure: **step-by-step instructions for an exercise that
can injure the reader's spine**, published with no medical disclaimer of any
kind.

The page is not vague about the risk. It has a section headed "**Risks & Safety
Concerns**", and two under it: "**Spinal Compression & Injury**" and "**Nerve
Damage & Chronic Injuries**". It tells readers that poor form risks "compression,
nerve irritation, and damage to the spine, discs, or ligaments due to axial and
shear forces". It then gives a numbered technique walkthrough, a "Sample Weekly
Routine" table, a "SixthSenseMMA style Plan (2–3 sessions/week)" and a
progression plan — everything a reader needs to attempt the movement at home,
unsupervised, having been told exactly what it can do to their neck.

Nowhere does it say: ask a doctor first; do not attempt this if you have an
existing neck or spinal condition; learn it with a coach watching. A reader with
an undiagnosed disc problem gets a weekly plan and no warning.

**Why this one is High rather than Medium.** The knife and self-defence posts
describe what the law might allow. This post instructs a reader to load their
cervical spine, acknowledges in its own words that doing so can cause nerve
damage and disc injury, and provides a schedule. If someone follows it and is
hurt, the page is the instruction that they followed, published by the business
that would have supervised them.

**What to do:** add a short, plain disclaimer at the top of `/neck-bridges/` and
above any future exercise-instruction content —

> This article is general information, not medical advice. Neck training loads
> the cervical spine. Speak to a physician before starting, especially if you
> have any history of neck, back or nerve problems, and learn these movements
> under qualified supervision.

— and have a coach or physiotherapist read the technique and progression
sections before they stay up. The same sentence pattern covers the law posts.

**A second post, about knives.** `/martial-blade-concepts/` goes further,
because the subject is a weapon. Two of its section headings are legal claims in
themselves — "**Learn Legal, Controlled Knife Usage**" and "**Disengagement &
Legal De-escalation**" — and the body under them tells readers they will learn
to use a blade within legal limits and when disengaging is the legally safer
choice.

Knife law varies far more than unarmed self-defence law does, and it varies on
details a class cannot generalise about: blade length, locking mechanisms,
concealed versus open carry, whether carry is lawful at all in a given city, and
whether defensive use of a knife counts as deadly force. A page that promises to
teach "legal" knife use, names no state and carries no disclaimer is making a
promise it cannot keep for every reader — and the gym is in Coppell, Texas,
while the page is readable anywhere.

**What to do, for both posts:** add a short line to each section saying the
information is general, is not legal advice, and that weapon and self-defence law
varies by state and city; and have the knife sections read by someone qualified
before they stay up. The client should get that from a lawyer in their state,
not from a rebuild note.

**In the rebuild:** both sections are reproduced word for word, with nothing
added, softened or caveated.

---

## SEO

### 4. Broken `%…%` page title — SEO — High — recurring template bug

The page title is wrapped in stray percent signs, a broken template variable
from an SEO plugin. It shows in Google results, browser tabs and social-share
previews as, for example,
`%Brazilian Jiu Jitsu Belt Ranks 2025: Path to Black Belt%`.

**Affected — 2 pages:**

| Post | Title as served | Published |
|---|---|---|
| `/brazilian-jiu-jitsu-belt-ranks/` | `%Brazilian Jiu Jitsu Belt Ranks 2025: Path to Black Belt%` | 2025-03-28 12:43 UTC |
| `/brazilian-jiu-jitsu-mat/` | `%Brazilian Jiu Jitsu Mat: Top 5 Reasons Gyms Go Premium%` | 2025-03-28 13:37 UTC |

On both pages all three title tags are broken: `<title>`, `og:title` and
`twitter:title`.

**This is a template bug, not a one-off typo.** It appears on two separate
posts, both published on the same day within an hour of each other, which
points to the SEO plugin's title template or a setting in use at that time
rather than a mistake typed into one post.

**Check done so far:** the `<title>`, `og:title` and `twitter:title` of all 63
live pages were scanned; only the two posts above are affected today.

**What to do:** find the title template or variable in the SEO plugin that
outputs the `%` signs and fix it there, then re-save both posts. Because the
cause is a template, **check every post** (including drafts, and any post
published or edited later) for the same `%…%` title, not only these two.

### 29. Content duplicated inside a post: a whole article on one, a whole section on another — CONTENT, SEO — High

**A ten-row technique table published word for word on two different posts.**
`/muay-boran-techniques/` and `/10-muay-boran-techniques/` both carry a "Specific
Top 10" section listing the same ten Muay Boran techniques with the **same ten
descriptions**, character for character — "Double uppercuts and snapping strike to
skull or chin", "Shoulder and elbow attack targeting temple and neck", "Arm
dislocation technique for close combat dominance" and the other seven.

This is a different shape of duplication from the cases below. Those are whole
articles or whole sections pasted into the wrong page. This is **one deliberate
asset reused on two competing URLs**, where the headings around it were rewritten
so the pages look distinct (issue 7). Of the two, `/10-muay-boran-techniques/`
expands each row into prose underneath; this page leaves the table to stand alone.

**In the rebuild** the table is reproduced exactly, promoted to a proper
`<thead>` with `<th scope="col">` and a row header on each of the ten rows, and
verified cell by cell against the live page. **What to do:** keep the table on
one page only, and have the other link to it.

**Three fragments of the Sambo beginner post survive on an MMA self-defence
page, including a sentence that calls the gym a "sambo academy".**
`/mix-martial-arts-for-self-defense/` was built from the same template as
`/sambo-techniques-for-beginners/`, and three pieces of that post's copy were
never swapped out:

| Fragment, as published | What it should say |
|---|---|
| "Located in Coppell Texas **this sambo academy and mma center** teaches effective self defense styles like krav maga bjj muay thai boxing wrestling and mma" | an MMA centre, not a Sambo academy |
| "Mats cushion falls **sambo gear beginners** or mma gloves get recommended for protection" | "sambo gear beginners" is a keyword phrase from the Sambo post, not English |
| "**Ukemi** rolls proper breathing and injury prevention build trust so learning feels secure" | ukemi is the Japanese breakfall term from the Sambo and judo curriculum |

**The first one is a service claim, not just a copy-paste.** "This sambo academy"
tells a reader the business is a Sambo academy, on a page about mixed martial
arts, for a discipline with no programme page — so it belongs to issue 39 as well
as here. It is the **seventh** page to carry a Sambo claim.

**The second shows how the copy is produced.** "Sambo gear beginners" is not a
phrase anyone writes; it is a search query with the words reordered, lifted
straight out of `/sambo-techniques-for-beginners/` where the same string appears.
Finding it on a second page means the January 2026 posts are being generated from
one another rather than from a brief.

**In the rebuild** all three are reproduced exactly, because they are published
text. **What to do:** search the January 2026 posts for "sambo", "ukemi" and
"gear beginners" and fix every hit; there may be more.

`/what-is-muay-thai/` contains the **entire** "Jiu Jitsu Guard Position"
article, pasted in above its own content. The Muay Thai article's own first
paragraph does not start until roughly two thirds of the way down the page.

| | Words |
|---|---|
| Whole page | about 5,980 |
| The duplicated Jiu Jitsu article | 3,481 |
| The actual Muay Thai article | 2,489 |

The duplicated block is word-for-word identical to `/jiu-jitsu-guard-position/`,
including its 15 H2 sections, its roughly 50 sub-headings, its three images and
its "Conclusion". So the page has two H1-level topics, two "Conclusion"
sections, and the Jiu Jitsu images appear on a Muay Thai page.

**Why it matters:**
- A reader who clicks "What Is Muay Thai?" reads about 3,500 words on BJJ guard
  positions before reaching a single word about Muay Thai. Most will leave.
- It is duplicate content against `/jiu-jitsu-guard-position/`: two URLs
  carrying the same 3,481 words, so search engines must pick one and may rank
  neither well.
- The page's topic is muddled. Its own keyword ("what is muay thai") is
  outweighed by guard-position text, which works against the ranking the post
  was written for.
- The FAQ shown on the page belongs to the Jiu Jitsu article, so the Muay Thai
  content has no FAQ of its own.

**What to do:** edit `/what-is-muay-thai/` and delete the duplicated block —
everything from the Jiu Jitsu opening question down to its "Conclusion"
paragraph — leaving the Muay Thai content that follows. Check the page in the
block editor: the Muay Thai text sits in separate blocks after it, so removing
the earlier ones is enough. Then check the other recent posts for the same
paste error.

**In the rebuild:** `dist/what-is-muay-thai.html` contains only the Muay Thai
article. The duplicated block, its three images and its FAQ were all left out,
and the post has no FAQ section as a result.

#### It has happened again, at section scale: `/self-defense-classes-near-me/`

The site's newest post publishes its **"Adult Self Defense Programs" section
twice**. Not a repeated heading — the whole section:

| | First copy | Second copy |
|---|---|---|
| H2 | Adult Self Defense Programs | Adult Self Defense Programs |
| H3s | Adult Brazilian Jiu Jitsu Fundamentals · Advanced Techniques & Strategy · Adult Muay Thai Training · Open Mat Sessions (Flexible Weekly Training) | *identical, all four* |
| Ordered list | 5 items | *identical, all five* |
| Words | 317 | 317 |
| Plain text | — | **word-for-word identical** |

The two copies differ in exactly one respect: the second has `<strong>` keyword
emphasis on phrases like "self defense classes near me" and "fitness" that the
first does not. That is the tell — someone produced a keyword-emphasised revision
of the section and pasted it in **instead of replacing** the original.

They are also not adjacent. The first sits between "Teen Self Defense Programs"
and "Women's Self Defense Classes"; the second sits between "Women's Self
Defense Classes" and "What to Expect in Our Classes". So the page's structure
reads: Kids → Teen → **Adult** → Women's → **Adult again** → What to Expect. A
reader scrolling through meets the adult programmes, then the women's
programmes, then the adult programmes again, identically worded.

**Three consequences.**

- **The reader loses trust.** Repeating 317 words verbatim is the most visible
  kind of carelessness there is; it is noticed immediately and it is impossible
  to read as anything but unchecked.
- **The page indexes badly.** Two identical H2s with the same text generate two
  identical anchor targets, and roughly 10% of the page's 2,973 words are a
  straight repeat.
- **The four-item section index at the top of the page lists "Adult Self Defense
  Programs" once**, so the page's own navigation does not match its contents.

**What to do:** delete the **first** copy and keep the second, which is the
keyword-emphasised revision that was presumably meant to replace it. Then check
the rest of the September posts for the same paste error — this is now twice, on
posts eight months apart, and both times it was a paste that added rather than
replaced.

**In the rebuild:** both copies are reproduced exactly as published, because the
brief is a faithful replica, and the build fails if either is silently removed.
This is the one place where the rebuild deliberately keeps a fault that a reader
will notice.

### 30. Bad tags: zero-width spaces, section titles used as tags, misspellings, and twenty-two on one post — SEO — Medium

**Eighteen tags, the largest set on the site, one of them a weapons claim and one
a misspelling.** `/muay-boran-techniques/` carries: "Ancient Muay Thai", "muay
boran", "Muay Boran Clinch", "Muay Boran Elbow Techniques", "Muay Boran Joint
Locks", "Muay Boran Knee Strikes", "muay boran moves", "Muay Boran Self Defense",
"Muay Boran Strikes", "Muay Boran Sweeps", "muay boran techniques", "Muay Boran
Throws", "Muay Boran Training", "**Muay Boran Weapons**", "muay chaiya", "muay
korat", "Muay Lopburi", "**Muay Tha Sao**".

**All eighteen name a discipline with no programme page.** Three points:

- **"Muay Boran Weapons"** is a weapons tag on a page that never mentions a
  weapon except "krabi krabong influences" — so it generates an indexable archive
  advertising Muay Boran weapons instruction that the page itself does not
  describe. It joins "Knife Fighting Training" and the Sambo submission tags as a
  tag that claims more than its page does.
- **"Muay Tha Sao"** is the style the page's own body spells "muay thasao",
  broken into three words. It is the fourth tag-level misspelling after "muat
  boran" and "diffrent", and like those it becomes the archive's permanent URL.
- The four style tags are **title-cased in the tag list and lower-cased in the
  body** — "Muay Lopburi" and "muay korat" as tags, "muay lopburi" and "muay
  korat" in the prose — so the page is inconsistent with itself.

That takes the running total to **82 untaught-discipline tags** across the Sambo,
Muay Boran, weapons and MMA posts. **In the rebuild** no tag pills are rendered on
any post. **What to do:** delete "Muay Boran Weapons" and "Muay Tha Sao" outright,
and keep three.

**Both spellings published as separate tags: "MMA Self Defence" AND "MMA Self
Defense".** `/mix-martial-arts-for-self-defense/` carries fourteen tags: "best
martial arts for self defense", "Brazilian Jiu Jitsu MMA", "Combat MMA", "Mixed
Martial Arts", "mixed martial arts training", "MMA Clinch Work", "MMA Fighting
Techniques", "mma for self defense", "MMA Grappling", "MMA Ground Game", "**MMA
Self Defence**", "**MMA Self Defense**", "MMA Street Fighting", "MMA Striking".

**Two of them are the same tag with one letter changed.** "MMA Self Defence" and
"MMA Self Defense" generate two separate public archives for one idea, splitting
whatever authority either would have had and giving Google two near-identical
thin pages. It is the tag-level version of the cluster's spelling split at issue
7, and the only place on the site where both forms are published side by side.

Three more points:

- **"MMA Street Fighting"** is a street-violence tag on a school that teaches
  children, the same fault as the firefighting and street tags at issue 51.
- **"best martial arts for self defense"** is, again, another post's primary
  keyword used as a tag (it belongs to
  `/best-martial-arts-for-self-defense/`), so the archive competes with the post.
- Capitalisation is inconsistent within the set: "Mixed Martial Arts" and "mixed
  martial arts training" sit beside each other, as do "mma for self defense" and
  "MMA Self Defense".

Unlike the Sambo and weapons sets, **none of these tags names an untaught
discipline** — they are all MMA or BJJ, which the gym does teach. **In the
rebuild** no tag pills are rendered on any post. **What to do:** delete "MMA Self
Defence" and "MMA Street Fighting", drop the duplicate keyword, and keep three.

**Seventeen weapons tags, one of them "Knife Fighting Training".**
`/martial-arts-weapons-training/` carries: "Arnis Martial Arts", "Bo Staff
Training", "Eskrima Stick Fighting", "Filipino martial arts weapons", "Japanese
Weapons Training", "Kali Weapons", "Kendo Sword Training", "**Knife Fighting
Training**", "Kobudo Weapons", "Krav Maga Weapons", "Kung Fu Weapons", "Martial
Arts Weapon Forms", "Martial Arts Weapons Training", "Nunchaku Training", "Sai
Weapons", "Samurai Weapons", "Sword Martial Arts".

**All seventeen name a weapon or a weapon system, and the gym has a programme page
for none of them.** Six are explicit training claims that generate their own
indexable archives — "Bo Staff **Training**", "Japanese Weapons **Training**",
"Kendo Sword **Training**", "Knife Fighting **Training**", "Nunchaku
**Training**", "Martial Arts Weapons **Training**".

**"Knife Fighting Training" is the one to delete first.** It is a public,
indexable tag archive on a martial arts school's website asserting that the school
trains knife fighting, on a page that states **no minimum age** and names teens
four times (issue 39). Of all 64 untaught-discipline tags now recorded across the
Sambo, Muay Boran and weapons posts, that is the single most exposed.

Four more disciplines also join the list at issue 39 through these tags alone:
**Kendo**, **Kobudo**, **Arnis** and **Sai** — none of them mentioned in any
programme, none with a page.

**In the rebuild** no tag pills are rendered on any post. **What to do:** delete
"Knife Fighting Training" regardless of what happens to the page; keep at most

**Seventeen tags on one post — the second-largest set on the site — and all

**Ten tags on one post, and every one of them is another post's primary keyword.**
`/best-martial-arts-for-self/` carries: "best martial arts for self defense",
"best self defense fighting styles", "Martial Arts for beginners self defense",
"martial arts for self defense", "martial arts self defense techniques", "real

**All ten are the same phrase reordered.** Strip the words and every one is a

arts for self defense"** is `/best-martial-arts-for-self-defense/`'s keyword and
**"martial arts for self defense"** is `/martial-arts-for-self-defense/`'s. So the

competing with each other (issue 7).

Two points in the client's favour: **no tag names a city** on this post, unlike
the thirty city-targeted tags above, and **no tag targets an untaught
discipline** — they are all about self-defence generally, which BJJ and Muay Thai

its slug.

**One capitalisation slip:** "**Martial Arts** for beginners self defense" is
title-cased mid-phrase while the other nine are lower case.

**In the rebuild** no tag pills are rendered on any post. **What to do:** two tags
cover this set — "self defense martial arts" and "martial arts for beginners" —
and the other eight should go with their archives.

**Fifteen tags on one post, eleven of them the same idea.**

Unlike `/muay-thai-gear-for-safety/` (issue 75), none of these belongs to a

- **Eleven are the same concept reworded**: attire, clothing, outfit, uniform,

- **"kickboxing outfit" targets a discipline with no programme page.** Kickboxing
  maps to the Muay Thai programme in practice, but the gym's five programmes name

- **Capitalisation is inconsistent within the set**: six are Title Case ("Muay Thai
  clothing", "Muay Thai gear", "Muay Thai outfit", "Muay Thai shorts") and nine are
  lower case, so the archive pages will be titled inconsistently.

**What to do:** cut to three or four — "what to wear to muay thai class", "muay
thai shorts", "muay thai beginner guide" — delete "kickboxing outfit" and "Muay
Thai clothing", and settle on one capitalisation. Then run the tag audit this

policy, so one post gets fifteen irrelevant terms and the next gets none. The

**A tag set that is on topic, and still makes issue 7 worse.** The ten tags on

Five of the ten — "combat sambo", "freestyle sambo", "self-defense sambo", "sport
sambo", "types of sambo" — are also the post's own five H3s and its H2, which is
the pattern logged below: section titles used as tags, each generating a thin
