| "free trial class" / "trial classes ... without commitment" | partly — the site's CTA says the first class is free |
| "Monthly packages offer unlimited access drop in rates and family discounts" | no (issue 55) |
| "No hidden fees appear and payment plans help long term students" | no |

**The page states no prices**, so nothing was invented and nothing needed omitting.

**2026-01 addition — and this one has its own H3.**
`/adult-beginner-martial-arts/` advertises it under the heading "Small Group &
One on One Coaching": "For those who prefer more focus **one on one coaching**
lets you move at your exact pace address specific goals and build confidence
faster." It is the most prominent placement yet — a named service with its own
section on a conversion page — and it is still confirmed by no programme page, no
price and no booking route.

### 74. Posts that sell equipment the site has no shop for — CONTENT (accuracy), TRUST — **CRITICAL, the client must confirm**

#### The clearest statement yet: "our shop", three named brands, and try-before-you-buy

`/kickboxing-gloves/` (26 December 2025) does not imply retail. It states it, six
times, in the gym's own voice:

> "We offer best kickboxing gloves for beginners **at our shop**."

> "**We stock** top kickboxing gloves brands like **everlast, venum, and
> hayabusa** for quality choices."

> "Members **try gloves for kickboxing in person at our Coppell TX gym before
> buying**."

> "Members get access to top kickboxing gloves brands such as everlast and venum
> **right at our gym shop**. **We carry premium lines** with durable synthetic
> leather and advanced foam padding for real hand protection."

> "**Our gym at 817 S MacArthur Blvd #100 stocks a full range** of kickboxing
> gloves **ready to try on the spot**."

> "**Ready to shop** for kickboxing gloves? Call (469) 972-7800..."

It adds sizing as a service — "we give **personal custom size guidance** to match
oz weight and fit", "**Size chart** helps pick the right glove from 16oz" — and an
H3 headed **"Local Kickboxing Gloves Near Me at Our Coppell TX Gym"**, which is a
retail search query turned into a section heading. One paragraph even positions the
shop against online sellers: members get the brands "without waiting for
shipping".

**Three brands are named: Everlast, Venum and Hayabusa**, two of them in H2s
("Muay Thai and Kickboxing Glove Brands Like Hayabusa and Venum", "Access to Top
Kickboxing Gloves Brands Like Everlast and Venum"), and the copy invites readers to
"try everlast classics or venum premium styles".

**What is NOT on the page:** no price, no size chart image, no cart, no checkout,
no product listing and **no link to any retailer** — the only external link on the
page is Facebook. So the page tells a reader to come to the gym and buy gloves and
gives them no way to see what is available or what it costs.

**And there is still no shop anywhere on the site.** The audit found no cart, no
checkout, no WooCommerce, no product pages and no price list across all 89 posts
and the six site pages. Three posts now sell equipment:
`/muay-thai-gear-for-safety/` (eight products, below),
`/mixed-martial-arts-training-gloves/` (a buying guide) and this one, which is the
only one to use the word "shop" about the business itself.

**This is the item on the list most likely to produce a wasted visit.** A reader
can act on it today: drive to 817 S MacArthur Blvd expecting to try Hayabusa gloves
and buy a pair. **The client must confirm whether equipment sales are a real
service.** If they are, the site needs a shop page with stock and prices, and
Product schema — the rebuild deliberately adds none. If they are not, every
sentence quoted above has to go, and the three posts need rewriting as
recommendation guides that point somewhere a reader can actually buy.

`/muay-thai-gear-for-safety/` is not written as a buying guide. It is written as
a **product catalogue for Sixth Sense MMA**. Its "Muay Thai Products" H2 opens:

> **We've got your muay thai gear covered with top picks. Our selection** brings
> authentic quality to every fighter from newbies to seasoned pros. Check out
> our gloves shorts and more each piece is designed for the gym or ring.
> **Let's look at what we offer.**

Then eight H3s, one per product, each in the first person with a purchase
instruction at the end:

| Section | Claim | Call to action |
|---|---|---|
| Gloves | "**Our gloves at Sixth Sense MMA** are built for action. Made with durable leather…" | "**Pick a pair** and feel the difference!" |
| Shorts | "**Our muay thai shorts at Sixth Sense MMA** are a game-changer… The designs come **straight from Bangkok**" | "**Pick your favorite color**… and hit the gym" |
| Shin Guards | "**ours at Sixth Sense MMA** don't disappoint. Made with tough padding…" | "**Grab a pair** and train worry-free!" |
| Headgear | "**Headgear from Sixth Sense MMA** keeps you safe in the ring" | "Try it out and feel the difference!" |
| Hand Wraps | "**ours at Sixth Sense MMA** deliver. Made from soft yet sturdy fabric…" | "**Get a set** of muay thai gloves and protect your hands today!" |
| Ankle Supports | "**Ankle supports at Sixth Sense MMA** keep you steady" | "Slip them on and move with ease!" |
| Mouthguards | "**Our mouthguards at Sixth Sense MMA** are a smart pick. Crafted from premium material…" | "**Grab one** and train with confidence!" |
| Groin Protectors | "**Groin protectors from Sixth Sense MMA** offer vital protection" | "Add this to your gear and stay safe!" |

**It names two brands as stocked**, under its own H2 "Why Pick Sixth Sense MMA
for Authentic Gear":

> **We stock Fairtex and Yokkao pieces** that pros swear by… **It's not just
> buying stuff**; it's grabbing tools like muay thai gloves that match the
> discipline we teach.

And the FAQ repeats it inside `FAQPage` structured data, where Google may quote
it as a direct answer:

> **Sixth Sense MMA offers authentic Muay Thai equipment from trusted brands
> like Fairtex and Yokkao.**

**There is no shop.** I searched the whole build and the live page: no shop,
store, product, cart or checkout page exists, no WooCommerce or add-to-cart
markup appears anywhere, and **the page states no price for any of the eight
items and gives no way to buy them.** Eight product sections, nine purchase
imperatives, zero prices, zero purchase routes.

**And the gym's other page on the same keyword says something different.**
`/muay-thai-gear/` — which targets "muay thai gear" as its primary keyword too
(issue 7) — describes the offering as **advice**, not retail:

> We offer **personalized gear advice** and professional training sessions at our
> physical gym

So two pages on one keyword disagree about whether the business sells equipment.
I checked the other gear posts — `/muay-thai-gloves/`, `/muay-thai-clothing/`,
`/muay-thai-gym-bag/`, `/muay-thai-backpack/`,
`/mixed-martial-arts-training-gloves/` — and none of them claims to stock or sell
anything; they are all generic advice. **This is the only page on the site that
claims to retail equipment.**

**Why this is CRITICAL rather than High.** Every other accuracy issue in this
document misdescribes a *service*. This one invents a **revenue line**. There are
only two possibilities and both need action today:

1. **The gym does not sell gear.** Then eight sections, two named brands and nine
   "grab one" instructions are advertising a shop that does not exist. A customer
   who walks in asking to buy the Fairtex shorts gets an awkward conversation,
   and "We stock Fairtex and Yokkao" is a straightforwardly false statement about
   the business.
2. **The gym does sell gear.** Then this is a real revenue line that appears on
   **one page out of seventy-eight**, with no prices, no product list, no shop
   page and no mention on the homepage or any programme page — and the page
   written to rank for the same keyword contradicts it. That is a commercial
   problem rather than an accuracy one, and a bigger missed opportunity than
   anything else in this document.

**What to do:**

- **If there is no retail:** rewrite all eight sections as "what to look for in
  gloves" rather than "our gloves", delete "We stock Fairtex and Yokkao", and fix
  the FAQ answer. The content is genuinely useful as a buying guide; it just
  cannot be written in the first person.
- **If there is retail:** build a shop page, put prices on it, link it from the
  homepage and the programme pages, and make `/muay-thai-gear/` agree with this
  page instead of contradicting it.

**In the rebuild:** every ownership claim, brand name and purchase imperative is
reproduced exactly as published, and the build fails if a price or a purchase
route is invented, or if "add to cart", "shop now" or similar appears anywhere.

---

### 75. Six tags about gym bags on a post about protective gear — SEO — **High**

`/muay-thai-gear-for-safety/` covers gloves, shorts, shin guards, headgear, hand
wraps, ankle supports, mouthguards and groin protectors. Its six tags are:

> best muay thai gym bag · muay thai equipment bag · Muay Thai gear backpack ·
> muay thai gear bag · Muay Thai Gym Bag · muay thai sports bag

**Every one is about bags and backpacks. The post contains no bag section at
all.** Not one of the six matches any heading, and the words "bag", "backpack"
and "storage" appear nowhere in its eight product sections.

**They belong to a different post — and two candidates already exist.**
`/muay-thai-gym-bag/` and `/muay-thai-backpack/` are both live and both are
actually about bags. The tag set reads as a copy-paste from one of them that
nobody looked at afterwards.

**What it costs.** A tag is an entry point: it exists so that someone searching
"best muay thai gym bag" finds this page. What they find is a page about
mouthguards and groin protectors, with no bag on it. That is a bounce, and it is
a bounce Google can see. It also means the two posts that *are* about bags are
now competing with this one for their own tag archives.

**One of the six carries a trailing zero-width space**, "muay thai gear bag" plus
U+200B — the same invisible character logged at issue 30, which creates a second
archive page distinct from the clean version of the same phrase.

**What to do:** delete all six and replace them with terms the post is actually
about — "muay thai gloves", "shin guards", "muay thai mouthguard", "muay thai
protective gear", "muay thai gear for beginners". Then check in WordPress whether
`/muay-thai-gym-bag/` and `/muay-thai-backpack/` still carry these terms themselves — if the
set was moved rather than copied, one of those posts may have been left without
its own tags.

**This is the clearest case yet for the tag audit recommended at issue 30.** Tags
on this site have been wrong in several ways — invisible characters, section
titles used as tags, arts the gym does not teach, a competitor's brand name — but
this is the first time an entire set belongs to a different article.

### 76. A whole article written as two- and three-word fragments rather than sentences — CONTENT, SEO — **CRITICAL**

Issue 67 logs four closing paragraphs of keyword salad on one post.
`/types-of-sambo/` is the same failure applied to **the entire article**: all
3,449 words of it, every section, from the opening line to the conclusion. This
is the post's first paragraph, quoted in full and unedited:

> Types of sambo start every journey at SixthSenseMMA. Sambo mixes old folk
> wrestling styles with judo throws. Sambo is a russian martial born for red
> army needs.

"Sambo is a russian martial" is not a phrase. It is the keyword "sambo is a
russian martial art" with its last word cut off, and the same truncation runs
through the whole post — "Sambo is a martial pro watch", "Sambo is a russian
martial expert guide", "Sambo is a martial full tool". The body of the Sport
Sambo section:

> Sport sambo bans strikes. Sport sambo focuses grapple. Sport sambo uses
> submission. Sport sambo loves throws. Wrestling style heavy. Freestyle
> wrestling close kin. Judo throws mix. Leg locks allowed. Types of leg locks
> heel hooks.

And the section on the gym's own training environment:

> SixthSenseMMA mats clean safe. Mma touch spar. Kickboxing pad flow. Sambo roll
> controlled. [...] Shin meet pad. Elbow graze check. Head strikes helmet on.

**What this is.** Every paragraph is a keyword followed by two or three words,
repeated until the paragraph is long enough, with a full stop after each. There
are 35 paragraphs on the page and they are all built this way. Nothing in the
article explains anything: a reader who wants to know what Sport Sambo is learns
that it "bans strikes", "focuses grapple" and "uses submission", in that
telegraphic register, and never gets a sentence.

**Why this is CRITICAL rather than a style note.**

- **It is unreadable, and it is a top-of-funnel page.** Someone searching "types
  of sambo" is at the research stage. This page answers nothing they asked, so
  it cannot convert them, and it is the first impression the gym makes.
- **It is the clearest machine-generated text on the site.** Issue 67's
  conclusion looks like a phrase list pasted into prose. This looks like output
  from a tool that was given a keyword list and a word count. The site is
  already carrying injected spam (issue 1) and an AI timestamp published in body
  copy (issue 70); a 3,449-word page of this is the kind of thing that moves a
  domain from "thin content" to "scaled content abuse" in Google's terms, which
  is a site-wide risk, not a page-level one.
- **It is also where issue 0 lives.** The four-sentence head-injury claim reads
  as just more of the same fragments, which is exactly why it got published and
  why nobody caught it. The format hides the content.

**What to do:** this page cannot be fixed by editing. Decide first whether it
should exist at all — its one subject is already a section of
`/sambo-martial-art/` (issue 7). If it stays, it needs rewriting from scratch,
in sentences, by someone who knows Sambo, and then it needs issue 0 resolved.
Then check how it was produced, because the same process is behind issue 67,
issue 70 and the keyword-crammed headings in issue 17.

---

**2026-02 addition — the same failure in a different shape: a phrase repeated
four times inside one clause.** `/kids-self-defense-classes-near-me/` publishes
this, as a sentence, in **six separate paragraphs**:

> Classes teach **self defense self defense self defense self defense training
> self defense techniques self defense classes**

"Self defense" appears four times in a row before the sentence reaches a verb. The
opening paragraph does the same thing and then runs straight into the
nine-discipline list, producing a 70-word stretch with no grammar in it at all:

> we offer Kids Self Defense Classes Near Me that teach real self defense self
> defense self defense self defense training self defense techniques self defense
> classes through kids martial arts kids karate kids mma brazilian jiu jitsu
> program jiu jitsu bjj brazilian jiu jitsu karate muay thai kickboxing muay thai
> classes muay thai kickboxing and krav maga in a safe positive space.

Other repeated blocks: "**self confidence self discipline self control**" (8
times), "**martial arts programs martial arts schools training center gym and
martial arts classes**" (6 times), "**get fit make friendship show personal
growth**" (5 times), and "**beginner kids teenager and even adult in classes for
adults**" (4 times) — the last of which is on a page about children.

**Reproduced verbatim**, as always. But this is a page a parent reads while
worried about their child, and the first paragraph is unreadable. Of the whole
audit this is the clearest case where the SEO damage and the human damage point
the same way: Google has penalised keyword stuffing for fifteen years, and a
parent will simply close the tab.

### 77. An eight-question FAQ where six questions are about boxing — SEO, CONTENT — **High**

`/types-of-sambo/` ends with an eight-question accordion. Two questions are
about its subject. The other six are not:

| # | Question | On topic? |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | "What are the different types of Sambo?" | Yes — the page's own subject |
| 2 | "What are the big 4 martial arts?" | No |
| 3 | "What is the no. 1 martial art?" | No |
| 4 | "Is Khabib a wrestler or Sambo?" | Yes — Sambo-related |
| 5 | "What are the 4 styles of boxing?" | No — boxing |
| 6 | "What is the hardest fighting style?" | No |
| 7 | "What is the 7 punch in boxing?" | No — boxing |
| 8 | "What is the hardest boxing style?" | No — boxing |

Three of the eight are about **boxing**, which the gym does not teach and this
post does not discuss. Questions 2, 3 and 6 are generic "best martial art"
queries, which belong to the seven-page cluster in issue 7 and pull this page
into competition with all of them.

**Why it matters:** these questions are marked up as an FAQ, so they are
eligible for rich results. If Google shows this page for "what is the 7 punch in
boxing", the click goes to a Sambo article from a gym that teaches neither
boxing nor Sambo, and bounces. It also dilutes what the page is about: five of
the eight questions tell Google this is a page about martial arts in general or
boxing in particular. This is a different fault from issue 9 (one generic FAQ
reused on 13 posts) and issue 42 (FAQ questions published as section headings) —
here the questions are unique to the page and simply belong to other subjects.

**What to do:** keep questions 1 and 4, delete the six that are off-topic, and
write four more about Sambo — which type suits a beginner, whether striking is
allowed, what a kurtka is, how Sambo compares with Judo and BJJ. Those are
questions the article's own sections already have answers for.

---

### 78. A local-intent post that publishes no address — SEO (local), CONTENT — Medium

`/types-of-sambo/` names Coppell four times in its own copy, including in a
"Contact Us" section that asks the reader to get in touch:

> SixthSenseMMA in Coppell, TX opens its doors for anyone ready to learn sambo.
> [...] Contact us at info@sixthsensemma.com or call **469-972-7800** to start.

There is no street address anywhere on the page. Every other recent post ends
with the full NAP block — "Located at 817 S MacArthur Blvd #100, Coppell, TX
75019" — and that block is how a local business page tells both a reader and a
search engine where it is. This one gives a city, an email and a phone number
and stops. The email address is also published as plain text rather than a
`mailto:` link, so a reader on a phone has to retype it.

**Why it matters:** the page asks for contact and then withholds the one detail
that lets someone visit. For local search, a page with city-level intent and no
address is weaker than the same page with one, and inconsistency across the blog
makes the address harder for Google to associate confidently with the brand. It
is a small fix with a direct effect.

**What to do:** add the standard NAP block to the Contact Us section, matching
the wording on the other posts exactly, and make the email address a
`mailto:` link. The rebuilt page reproduces the omission rather than silently
adding an address, so the two sides still match.

---

### 79. One Sambo photo batch illustrates eight posts, seven of them unrelated — CONTENT (asset management), SEO — Medium

The November 2025 upload batch `Types-of-sambo-1` through `Types-of-sambo-10` was
uploaded for `/types-of-sambo/`. It is now the entire image set for three posts
on three different subjects:

| Post | Files used | Featured image |
|---|---|---|
| `/types-of-sambo/` | `-1`, `-2`, `-3-1` | `Types-of-sambo-1.jpg` |
| `/mixed-martial-arts-vs-kickboxing/` | `-4`, `-7`, `-6` | `Types-of-sambo-4.jpg` |
| `/what-to-wear-to-muay-thai-class/` | `-8`, `-9`, `-10` | `Types-of-sambo-8.jpg` |
| `/sambo-moves/` | `-11`, `-12-1`, plus `Sambo-moves-.jpg` | `Sambo-moves-.jpg` |
| `/top-fighting-styles/` | `-13`, `-14`, `-15` | `Types-of-sambo-13.jpg` |
| `/do-neck-bridges-build-muscle/` | `-16`, `-17`, `-18` | `Types-of-sambo-16.jpg` |
| `/martial-arts-progression/` | `-19`, `-20`, `-21` | `Types-of-sambo-19-1024x536.jpg` |
| `/are-kidney-shots-legal-in-ufc/` | `-22`, `-23`, `-24` | `Types-of-sambo-22-1024x536.jpg` |

The batch now runs to twenty-four files across eight posts, and only `/types-of-sambo/`
and `/sambo-moves/` are about Sambo at all. An article about whether kidney strikes
are legal in the UFC is illustrated with `Types-of-sambo-22`, `-23` and `-24`. An article about whether neck bridges
build muscle is illustrated with `Types-of-sambo-16.jpg`, `-17` and `-18`, and
unlike the five before it this post uses the full-size uploads rather than the
1024px resizes, so the pages are not even consistent in which file they request. A post about BJJ being the best
fighting style is illustrated with `Types-of-sambo-13.jpg`. Its featured image is the one exception to the naming
pattern and has a fault of its own: it is published as **`Sambo-moves-.jpg`**,
with a trailing hyphen before the extension, which is what WordPress produces when
a filename is generated from a title and the last word is dropped.

The files are genuinely different photographs — verified by md5, all twelve are
distinct — so this is not the duplicate-image fault of issue 26. It is a naming and
sourcing fault with three consequences:

- **Every filename is wrong for its page.** A post about what to wear to a Muay
  Thai class has a featured image called `Types-of-sambo-8.jpg`. Filenames are a
  ranking signal for image search, and all three posts are telling Google their
  pictures are about Sambo.
- **Nobody can find anything in the media library.** Ten files named for one post
  now belong to three, so replacing the Muay Thai apparel photo means knowing that
  it is `Types-of-sambo-9`.
- **It points at how these posts were made.** Eight articles published between
  11 and 17 November 2025 drew their illustrations from one batch, seven of them
  from a batch uploaded for a different post. The last two, \`/do-neck-bridges-build-muscle/\`
  and \`/martial-arts-progression/\`, went up 56 minutes apart. Two of the five, `/sambo-moves/` and
  `/top-fighting-styles/`, were published five minutes apart from one template
  (issue 80). Combined with issue 0 — the same four safety sentences on two of those
  three posts — and the fragment prose of issue 76, this looks like one production
  run rather than three editorial decisions.

**What to do:** re-upload each post's images under descriptive filenames and
repoint the three posts. If the photographs are stock or reused from a shoot, say
so in the media library so the next person knows. Then look at what produced these
three posts, which issue 0 also asks for.

**In the rebuild** the files are renamed to match their pages —
`sambo-types-01..03`, `mma-vs-kickboxing-01..03`, `what-to-wear-01..03` — and each
set is md5-checked against every other image in `dist/images/` before it is
placed, so the live naming fault is fixed rather than carried over.

---

### 80. A "Top Fighting Styles" post that ranks one discipline, built from another post's template — CONTENT, SEO — **High**

`/top-fighting-styles/` is titled for a ranked comparison and its meta description
promises "the top fighting styles in the world. From MMA to Muay Thai". It ranks
**one** discipline.

Its first H2, "Top Fighting Styles Programs at Sixth Sense Martial Arts", carries
four H3s:

| H3 | Discipline |
|---|---|
| Brazilian Jiu-Jitsu (BJJ) – All Levels | BJJ |
| Kids BJJ Programs (Little &amp; Rising Champions) | BJJ |
| Teen &amp; Adult BJJ Classes (Unlimited 60-Min) | BJJ |
| Family BJJ Programs | BJJ |

Four slots, one discipline, split by age group. This is the padding pattern
already logged for `/top-ten-martial-arts-for-self-defense/` (issue 65), where ten
slots held five disciplines counted twice — but there the padding at least used
five. Here it uses one.

**Every other style on the page is named only to be dismissed.** The page names
Muay Thai, MMA, boxing, kickboxing, Krav Maga, karate, judo, taekwondo, wrestling
and kung fu, and each appears as something BJJ defeats:

> "It differs from muay thai or thai boxing which use punch and kick."
> "**No kickboxing or karate high kicks here.**"
> "BJJ handles combat situations better than taekwondo or kung fu."
> "Those korean martial art and chinese martial art focus on stands up moves."
> "BJJ handles real combat better than muay thai kick or karate punch."
> "Krav maga is an israeli quick art but our BJJ builds full control."
> "**BJJ beats taekwondo spins here.** Taekwondo is a korean martial for show but
> BJJ wins combat situations."

**It contradicts the gym's own programme pages.** One FAQ answer states
"**Sixth Sense Martial Arts focuses only on BJJ for real control.**" The business
runs `/adult-muay-thai-classes/` and `/teen-muay-thai-classes/` — two of its five
programme pages are Muay Thai. A searcher comparing styles is told, on the gym's
own domain, that it does not really teach one of the two things it sells, and that
Muay Thai is the weaker choice. "No kickboxing or karate high kicks here" reads as
a selling point and is an argument against the Muay Thai programme.

**The page is also `/sambo-moves/` with the discipline swapped.** The two were
published two days apart, 13 November 2025, 20:40 and 20:45. Twelve of this post's
twenty-eight H3s are identical, in the same order, to twelve on `/sambo-moves/`:

> Build Strong Self-Defense · Get Fit and Strong · Win in Competitions · Learn Real
> MMA Skills · Boost Confidence · Fun for Kids and Adults

> Free Trial Class · Expert Coaches · Small Groups · Safe &amp; Fun Training ·
> Coppell's Top Gym · Kids &amp; Adults Programs

Both also run H2s of the form "Why Learn X at …", "Benefits of Learning X at …",
"Who Can Learn X at …", "Why Choose … for X". It is one template filled twice,
five minutes apart, once with Sambo and once with BJJ. `/sambo-moves/` claims the
gym teaches Sambo; this one claims it teaches only BJJ. **The two contradict each
other and were produced in the same sitting.**

**What to do:** either make the page a real comparison — name the styles, say what
each is good for, and link the gym's own Muay Thai programme where it is the
better answer — or retitle it as a BJJ page and 301 it. It cannot stay as a
"top styles" page that disparages the business's other programme. Then look at the
template: issue 7 now has eight pages chasing this query, and this one was
generated rather than written.

---

### 81. Five FAQ questions about Roblox video games — SEO, CONTENT — **High**

The FAQ on `/top-fighting-styles/` has nine questions. **Five are about video
games, not martial arts:**

| # | Question | Subject |
|---|---|---|
| 5 | "How to get fighting styles in baddies?" | *Baddies*, a Roblox game |
| 6 | "Where to get fighting styles?" | game phrasing, answered about the gym |
| 7 | "What are the best fighting styles in blox fruits?" | *Blox Fruits*, a Roblox game |
| 8 | "What's the best fighting style in blox fruits?" | *Blox Fruits* |
| 9 | "How to buy fighting styles in blox fruits?" | *Blox Fruits* |

*Blox Fruits* is one of the most-played games on Roblox; "fighting styles" there
are in-game purchases. Someone searching that phrase is a child looking for game
advice. The answers acknowledge it and pivot to selling classes:

> "Game meta changes. **Real top fighting styles stay BJJ.** Sixth Sense Martial
> Arts teaches BJJ for ground fighting control. [...] **Free trial proves BJJ beats
> virtual moves.**"

> "How to buy fighting styles in blox fruits? **Virtual currency only.** Real BJJ
> training costs nothing to try."

**Why this is High.**

- **It is keyword scraping, visible to the reader.** Someone comparing gyms sees a
  martial arts school answering questions about a Roblox game. It reads as
  unserious, on a page whose job is to establish expertise.
- **It is marked up as FAQPage.** These questions are eligible for rich results, so
  the gym's page can surface for "how to buy fighting styles in blox fruits" and
  take a click from a child who wants game help. That traffic cannot convert and
  the bounce is immediate.
- **It targets children with a sales pitch.** The answers tell a young reader their
  game is not real and that they should book a free trial instead. That is a
  separate judgement call for the client, but it should be a deliberate one.
- **It dilutes the page.** Five of nine questions tell Google this page is about
  games. The page is already the eighth in the self-defence cluster (issue 7) and
  needs every signal it can get.

**What to do:** delete all five. Keep questions 1-4, which are real martial arts
questions, and add Sambo-free, game-free replacements the article answers — what
BJJ costs to try, what a first class involves, what to wear. Then check the other
posts' FAQs for the same scraping: this is the third distinct FAQ fault in the
document, after the generic set reused on 13 posts (issue 9) and the six off-topic
boxing questions on `/types-of-sambo/` (issue 77).

---

### 82. A second H1 published in the middle of an article — SEO — Medium

`/do-neck-bridges-build-muscle/` publishes **two H1 elements**. The first is the
article title. The second sits two thirds of the way down, where every other post
on the site uses an H2:

```html
<h1>Why Choose Sixth Sense for Neck Bridges</h1>
```

It is the only in-body H1 found across all 85 posts. Every sibling section on the
same page — "What Are Neck Bridges?", "How Neck Bridges Work", "Benefits of Neck
Bridges", "Contact Us", "Conclusion" — is an H2, so this is a single mis-set block
rather than a template fault.

**Why it matters:** the H1 is the strongest on-page signal of what a document is
about, and a page with two tells a crawler the article has two subjects — "Do Neck
Bridges Build Muscle" and "Why Choose Sixth Sense". It also breaks the document
outline for screen readers, which use heading level to convey nesting: a listener
tabbing the headings hears a new top-level document begin halfway through the
article.

**In the rebuild** the text and its position are reproduced exactly and the element
is published as `<h2 id="why-choose-sixth-sense-for-neck-bridges">`, so the page
carries one H1 as the project requires and the section still appears in the table
of contents with every other H2. The build asserts the live page still has two H1s,
so if the client fixes it the rebuild will flag the change rather than drift.

**What to do:** change it to an H2 in WordPress. One edit.

---

### 83. A rules explainer that contradicts itself, and says kidney punches are legal in boxing — CONTENT (accuracy) — **High**

`/are-kidney-shots-legal-in-ufc/` exists to answer one question about the rules.
Three of its rule statements are wrong or self-cancelling, and they are reproduced
exactly in the rebuild rather than corrected.

**1. It says the Unified Rules forbid kidney strikes, then says they do not.** Two
consecutive sentences under "Unified Rules of Mixed Martial Arts":

> The Unified Rules clearly list strikes to the back of the head spine and
> **kidneys** as fouls. Rule 13 bans hitting the spine or the back of the torso at
> any time. **The kidney area on the side remains open for legal attack** because
> it is not considered the back.

The first sentence puts kidneys on the fouls list. The third says kidney strikes
are legal. The page's entire thesis is the third one — the title, the H2s and the
referee table all say side kidney strikes are permitted — so the first sentence is
simply wrong, and it is the one a reader skimming for the answer is most likely to
land on.

**2. It says kidney punches are "fully legal in boxing".** Under "Body Uppercuts to
the Kidney Area":

> From the clinch or double underhooks short body uppercuts to the kidney area are
> common and **fully legal in boxing or MMA rules** when they land on the side.

Boxing prohibits kidney punches. They are a foul under the unified boxing rules
that the major commissions and sanctioning bodies apply, which is why referees
warn for them and why the term "kidney punch" is a standard foul call in boxing
commentary. Whatever the MMA position, the boxing half of that sentence is
incorrect on a page whose job is to state what is allowed.

**3. "Rule 13" is cited with no source.** The page attributes the spine prohibition
to "Rule 13" of the Unified Rules and names "state athletic commissions" generally.
**It links neither.** In fact the page carries **no outbound source link at all** —
its only external link is to Facebook. A rules explainer that cites a numbered rule
and an unnamed commission without linking either gives a reader no way to check it,
and gives the page no authority signal for a query where authority is the whole
value.

**Why this is High.** This is the one page on the site whose only purpose is factual
accuracy about regulation. A reader comes to it to settle an argument. It
contradicts itself in the first paragraph of its rules section, states a boxing rule
backwards, and sources nothing. It is also the page recommended at issue 47 as the
model for rewriting `/kidney-punch/`, so these three faults need fixing before it
takes on that role.

**What to do:** delete "and kidneys" from the first sentence, or rewrite it as
"strikes to the back of the head and spine"; correct or remove the boxing claim;
and link the Unified Rules text on the relevant commission's site. All three are
single-sentence edits.

---

### 84. An all-caps brand suffix in a title tag, and 23 H2s to 6 H3s — SEO — Medium

Two presentation faults on `/kickboxing-gloves/`, both reproduced in the rebuild.

**1. The title tag shouts the brand.**

```html
<title>Kickboxing Gloves Best Quality &amp; Protection BY SIXTH SENSE</title>
```

**"BY SIXTH SENSE"** in capitals, where every other post on the site writes the
brand normally in its title ("… by Sixth Sense MMA", "… | Sixth Sense Martial
Arts"). The page's own H1 writes it properly: "Kickboxing Gloves Enhance Your Muay
Thai and Training **at Sixth Sense MMA**".

In a search result the capitals read as shouting, which measurably depresses click
rate, and some engines rewrite titles they judge to be over-optimised — so the
suffix may not even appear. It is also the truncated brand: "Sixth Sense" without
"MMA" or "Martial Arts", which is a twelfth spelling for issue 33's list.

**2. Nearly every section is an H2.** The page has **23 H2s and 6 H3s**, and the
levels do not reflect the structure. For example:

| Published level | Heading | What it actually is |
|---|---|---|
| H2 | "Common Problems with Kickboxing Gloves" | a real section |
| H3 | "Poor Fit and Wrist Protection…" | its subsection — correct |
| **H2** | "Inadequate Padding and Knuckle Protection Causing Hand Injuries" | **also a problem, so also a subsection — but published a level up** |
| H2 | "Solutions Provided by Sixth Sense MMA" | a real section |
| H3 | "Best Kickboxing Gloves for Beginners…" | its subsection — correct |
| **H2** | "Kickboxing Training Gloves for Heavy Bag…", "Muay Thai and Kickboxing Glove Brands…", "Custom Size Guidance…", "Hand Wraps…", "Best Kickboxing Gloves for Women…" | **five more solutions, all flattened to the top level** |
| H2 | "Case Studies from Sixth Sense MMA" | a real section |
| H3 | "Beginner Boxer Builds Confidence…" | its subsection — correct |
| **H2** | "Advanced Fighter Improves Sparring…", "Women's Experience with Kickboxing Gloves…" | **the other two case studies, flattened** |

Three sections start correctly with one H3 and then promote every sibling to H2.
The result is a document outline with no nesting: a screen reader announces 23
top-level sections, and the rebuilt page's table of contents has **22 entries**,
the longest in the project, because it lists H2s only.

**What to do:** demote the flattened subsections to H3 — about eleven headings —
and lower-case the title suffix to "by Sixth Sense MMA". Both are mechanical edits.
The rebuild reproduces the levels as published, so the outline will fix itself here
once the live page is corrected.

---

### 85. A twelve-question FAQ where ten questions are celebrity trivia — SEO, CONTENT — **High**

`/bjj-belt-ranks-explained/` closes with twelve FAQ questions. **Ten of them ask
what belt a famous person holds**, and two of those ask the same thing twice.

| # | Question | About the belt system? |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | What are the BJJ belts in order? | yes |
| 2 | What level of BJJ is Mark Zuckerberg? | no |
| 3 | What belt rank is Keanu Reeves? | no |
| 4 | What is the 80 20 rule in BJJ? | no — unrelated training maxim |
| 5 | Is Keanu Reeves a white belt? | no — **duplicate of #3** |
| 6 | What's better, BJJ or jjj? | no |
| 7 | What belt is John Wick? | no — **a fictional character**, answered "has no official belt rank" |
| 8 | What belt is Elon Musk? | no |
| 9 | What rank is Conor McGregor in BJJ? | no |
| 10 | Is Tom Cruise a black belt? | no |
| 11 | What rank is Jason Statham in BJJ? | no |
| 12 | What belt is Jim Carrey? | no |

This is the same failure as issue 77 (an eight-question FAQ where six were about
boxing) and issue 81 (five questions about Roblox), and it is now the third
instance, so the FAQ block is being filled from a keyword tool rather than from
questions this gym's prospects actually ask.

**Two further problems with the answers themselves.** They are reproduced verbatim
and have not been corrected:

- Several state unverified facts about named living people as fact — Jason Statham
  "is a purple belt", "trained consistently under Renzo Gracie and Roger Gracie for
  over fifteen years"; Jim Carrey "is a brown belt ... has trained under Royce
  Gracie for many years"; Tom Cruise, Keanu Reeves and Elon Musk each assigned or
  denied a rank. These are claims about real third parties published on a
  commercial site; if any is wrong it is the client's exposure, not Google's.
- Question 7 asks the belt rank of a fictional character, and the answer explains
  that fictional characters do not have belt ranks. That exchange has no plausible
  search value.

**What to do.** Keep question 1. Replace the rest with questions about this gym:
how long promotions take here, whether there is a grading fee, what the kids belt
path is, and at what age a child moves to adult classes — the last of which the
site currently answers five different ways (issue 72).

## CHILD SAFETY CONTENT

### 86. A page advising parents of bullied children: what it promises, what it cites, and what it never tells a child to do — CONTENT (accuracy), SAFEGUARDING — **High, the client must review before publication**

`/anti-bullying-martial-arts-for-kids/` (26 February 2026, 3,577 words) is the
first page on this site written for a parent whose child is being bullied. That
makes it different from the rest of the audit: a parent reading it is worried, is
looking for reassurance, and will act on what it says. Everything below is
**quoted exactly as published** and **reproduced verbatim on the rebuilt page**
except the two sentences logged at issue 0.

#### a) Claims that training will stop bullying or that a child can protect themselves

**No sentence guarantees bullying will stop.** The strongest claims are
probabilistic, and there are seven of them:

| Where | Exact wording |
|---|---|
| Body | "This training makes them **less likely to be targeted** because bullies pick on kids who seem unaware or easy to push" |
| Body | "This makes them **less likely to be targeted** by bullies" |
| Body | "This smart approach makes sure every kid gets what they need to **prevent bullying** deal with bullies and feel ready" |
| Body | "children develop the ability to **defend themselves against bullies without starting a fight**" |
| Body | "They understand they **can defend themselves against bullies** without needing to hurt anyone" |
| FAQ 1 | "This approach **helps prevent bullying** while giving practical tools if needed" |
| FAQ 8 | "Many parents and experts say jiu jitsu kids stand their ground more often, walk away stronger and **rarely face repeated bullying**" |

**"Rarely face repeated bullying" is the one to look at.** It is the closest the
page comes to an outcome guarantee, it is attributed to "parents and experts" who
are not named, and it is in an FAQ answer rather than the body, where a parent
scanning for an answer is most likely to find it.

#### b) Advice toward physical response rather than de-escalation

**The page is strongly and consistently non-violent, and this is its best
feature.** De-escalation is the first thing taught and the last thing recommended:

- "The main point is **conflict avoidance**. Children learn to **choose peaceful
  paths first**."
- "Martial arts for self defense stresses that **the best defense is never needing
  to fight**."
- "They understand that **walking away asking for help or changing the subject**
  often stops problems fast."
- "All levels use brazilian jiu jitsu moves that fit small bodies **no punches and
  kicks needed**."
- "kids learn real self defense skills **without needing punches and kicks**"
- "**avoid fights when possible**" (FAQ 8)

**One sentence describes a physical response, and it is a restraint, not a
strike:** "They learn how to use leverage to escape pressure or **pin an attacker
until help arrives**." That is the only passage telling a child to hold another
child down. It is framed as holding until an adult comes, which is the safer
framing, but it is worth the client's eye because a child pinning another child in
a playground is a scene a school will read differently from a gym.

**Nothing on the page tells a child to hit back, strike first, or retaliate.**

#### c) Statistics and sources

**No statistic appears anywhere on the page.** No percentage, no figure, no
prevalence rate. **But two research-shaped claims are made with no source:**

| Where | Exact wording | Source cited? |
|---|---|---|
| FAQ 2 | "**Studies and parent reports show** trained kids face less bullying because they project quiet strength" | **no** |
| FAQ 8 | "**Many parents and experts say** jiu jitsu kids … rarely face repeated bullying" | **no** |

This is the pattern already logged at issue 62 (unnamed "experts" and unspecified
"studies"), now on a page about children. "Studies show" with no study named is
the single easiest claim for a competitor, a parent or a journalist to challenge.

#### d) Psychological and developmental claims

**The body's claims are ordinary confidence copy** — "Over time **low self esteem
fades** and a strong sense of worth takes its place", "Anti Bullying Martial Arts
for Kids **directly fights that fear**", "This knowledge **cuts worry**", an H3
called "**Reduced Fear and Anxiety in School Settings**", and "Parents notice
their child … **worries less about bullies**".

**The FAQ goes considerably further, into clinical territory, on a page selling
classes to parents:**

| FAQ | Exact wording |
|---|---|
| 3 | "Martial arts can be **very good for kids with behavioral problems** … Kids practice emotional self control … which **helps manage impulses**" |
| 3 | "**Many instructors see improvements in behavior** at home and school" |
| 5 | "Both karate and taekwondo **help kids with ADHD** but taekwondo often stands out slightly more" |
| 5 | "The structured routines belt system and emphasis on self control **help with impulsivity and attention**" |
| 5 | "Both **improve concentration, reduce hyperactivity** and teach patience" |
| 10 | "taekwondo karate and brazilian jiu jitsu **help many kids with ADHD**" |
| 10 | "structured sequences that **improve focus and reduce hyperactivity**" |

**Three of the ten FAQ answers make claims about a diagnosed neurodevelopmental
condition**, and two of them recommend a specific discipline for it. ADHD is a
clinical diagnosis; "reduce hyperactivity" is a treatment claim. No source, no
clinician and no caveat appears anywhere, and the page carries no disclaimer. This
belongs with the liability group at issue 31 as well as here.

#### e) Injury and safety claims

Two sentences were **omitted** and are recorded at issue 0: "without any risk of
getting hurt" and "without danger". The rest is qualified and was kept: "kids
don't face **unnecessary risks**", "Equipment checks happen regularly so nothing
breaks or wears out in a way that **could cause injury**" (an acknowledgement of
risk, correctly phrased), "Drills stay slow at first then build **only as fast as
the child is ready**", "everyone **taps early** to stop anything uncomfortable",
and "**No child ever trains alone** or without adult eyes on them".

#### f) Ages, and how they contradict the gym's own programme

**The page publishes three age bands. None of them matches the programme pages.**

| Source | Bands |
|---|---|
| `/anti-bullying-martial-arts-for-kids/` | **Young Kids (Ages 4–6)**, **Junior (Ages 7–10)**, **Pre Teen and Teen (Ages 11–15)** |
| `/` (homepage) | Kids **Ages 4 to 12**; **Little Champions Ages 4 to 7** |
| `/kids-bjj-classes/`, `/teen-bjj-classes/` | **no ages stated at all** |
| `/martial-arts-for-kids-strenght/` | "**ages 7 and up**" (issue 69) |
| `/martial-arts-classes/` | "starts at **ages 5 7**" |

Three specific conflicts:

1. **Little Champions is 4–7 on the homepage and 4–6 here.** The page even uses the
   programme name — "**Little champions** in kids martial arts learn through games
   songs and simple play" — under a heading that says Ages 4–6. A seven-year-old is
   a Little Champion on the homepage and a Junior on this page.
2. **Rising Champions gets the same treatment.** "**Rising champions** dive deeper
   into bjj moves" sits under Ages 7–10, and no other page states an age for it.
3. **The kids programme ends at 12 on the homepage and at 15 here**, with no teen
   programme named, although `/teen-bjj-classes/` exists.

**This is now the sixth different set of ages published for the same classes.**
Nothing has been corrected; the bands are reproduced exactly as published.

#### g) Parents, teachers and the school

**This is the gap, and it is the most important finding in this entry.** A page
about a bullied child, 3,577 words long, **never tells the child to tell a teacher,
tell a parent, or report it to the school.**

The nearest it comes is three generic phrases: "walking away **asking for help** or
changing the subject", "choose when to walk away or **get help**", and "pin an
attacker **until help arrives**". None names an adult. None names a teacher, a
parent, a counsellor or the school.

Teachers appear five times on the page and **every one of them is a martial arts
instructor or a generic reference to classroom listening** — "listening to
teachers" (about homework focus), "**Teachers** guide each move", "the **teachers**
explain patiently", and two mentions inside FAQ answers about choosing a gym.

Parents appear throughout, but **only as customers**: they "notice their child
feels less helpless", "watch their child grow", "appreciate knowing their child
learns from adults who care", and "fill out a quick form online". The page never
suggests a parent should contact the school.

**Every recognised anti-bullying framework puts telling an adult first.** A
commercial page that teaches a child what to do about bullying and omits that step
is a safeguarding gap, regardless of how non-violent the rest of the advice is. It
is also a reputational exposure: the omission is the first thing a critical reader
would notice.

**Recommended addition, for the client to write:** one paragraph in the
"Situational Awareness and Conflict Avoidance" section saying that telling a
teacher, a parent or another trusted adult is the first step, and that the gym's
training supports that rather than replacing it. **Nothing has been added to the
rebuilt page** — writing safeguarding copy on a client's behalf is the client's
call.

#### No charity, helpline or government resource is linked

The page cites **no anti-bullying charity, helpline or government resource**. Its
only outbound link is Facebook. For a page on this subject that is a missed
opportunity rather than a defect, but a single link to a recognised national
resource would cost nothing and would do more for the page's credibility than any
of its keyword repetition.

#### The free-trial link points at a thank-you page

The "**free trial class**" anchor — the single most important link on a conversion
page for worried parents — points at `https://sixthsensemma.com/thank-you/`, the
post-submission confirmation page. A parent who clicks it is told thank you for
something they have not done, and is given no form. **In the rebuild it points at
`kids-bjj-classes.html`**, the programme page that actually books a kids free
trial; the substitution is recorded here rather than made silently.

#### Images: two of the three show identifiable young faces, and two show the wrong art

| File | What it shows |
|---|---|
| `anti-bullying-01.jpg` (featured) | **An identifiable child**, roughly 9–12, face fully visible, in a BJJ gi, grappling on blue competition mats |
| `anti-bullying-02.jpg` | **An identifiable adolescent**, face fully visible, in a **karate** gi throwing a punch, with an adult black belt behind |
| `anti-bullying-03.jpg` | A large **adult karate class**, roughly fifteen adults in a traditional dojo — **no children at all** |

**Two points for the client.**

1. **Photo consent.** Two of the three images show the faces of minors. Images of
   children carry their own permissions requirement, separate from any stock
   licence. **The client should confirm that signed photo consent is on file for
   each identifiable child**, or replace the images. This applies whether or not
   the pictures were taken at this gym — and image 1, on blue competition mats with
   a club patch, may well have been.
2. **Two of the three are the wrong discipline.** The page sells Brazilian jiu
   jitsu and says twice that "no punches and kicks" are needed. Image 2 is a karate
   punch. Image 3 is a room of adults in karate stances, illustrating a section
   titled "Beginner to Advanced **Levels**" on a page about children. Neither
   appears to be the Coppell facility.

### 87. The second child-safety page: a parent FAQ that answers budgeting questions, teaches eye strikes at home, and tells a Texas reader what the law is in Pakistan — CONTENT (accuracy), SAFEGUARDING — **High, the client must review before publication**

`/kids-self-defense-classes-near-me/` (26 February 2026, 3,694 words) is the
**second page in this group**, published the same day as
`/anti-bullying-martial-arts-for-kids/` and three minutes after it. Everything
below is **quoted exactly as published** and **reproduced verbatim on the rebuilt
page** except the two sentences logged at issue 0.

#### a) Claims that training keeps a child safe or guarantees protection

**No outright guarantee, but the page leans harder than the bullying one.** The
repeated promise is capability rather than outcome:

| Where | Exact wording |
|---|---|
| Opening | "give children practical tools to **stay safe**" |
| Age groups | "feel ready to **defend themselves**" |
| Teen section | "the quiet assurance that comes from **knowing they can defend themselves well**" |
| Benefits | "knowing they **can defend themselves if needed**" |
| Curriculum | "helps kids of all ages **defend against attacker opponent stay safe**" |
| Why choose us | "learn to **defend themselves with practical tools that work in real life**" |

"Practical tools that work in real life" and "knowing they can defend themselves
well" are the two to look at: both tell a parent the training will function
against a real attacker, and the page's own threat model (below) includes adults.

#### b) Advice toward a child physically fighting

**The body keeps the escape-and-run framing**, which is correct: "They teach kids
to **yell run to safety and get help** right after breaking free"; "learn to **say
no or run if needed**"; "learn to **yell for help**"; "control a situation
**without hurting others more than needed**".

**The FAQ does not.** FAQ 4, "How to teach kids self defense?", instructs the
*parent* to coach the child at home:

> Begin with the "fight or flight" stance: feet apart, hands up for protection.
> Teach simple moves like **proper punching (fist tight aim for weak spots)**
> yelling for help and escaping grabs. Use role playing scenarios emphasize using
> voice assertively (e.g. shouting "No!") and **practice techniques like eye
> strikes or groin kicks** in a safe environment.

**This is the most specific violent instruction published to a child audience on
this site.** Eye strikes and groin kicks are not taught in any kids programme, are
not mentioned anywhere in the body of this page, and are given to parents as home
practice with no supervision, no age floor and no caveat. It is the same failure
as the elbow-strike home-practice passage already logged at issue 0, except the
target here is a child's eyes and the instruction is for a four-year-old's parent
to follow.

**The threat model includes adults.** The page refers to an "**attacker**" eleven
times and an "opponent" seven, and the teen section describes "escapes from
**stronger holds**" and "responding fast if needed". It never says the attacker is
another child.

#### c) Stranger danger, abduction and assault; statistics

**No statistic of any kind appears**, and no source is cited for anything. The
only percentages on the page are in FAQ 8, which is about **budgeting**
("allocate 50% of allowance… 30% to wants… 20% to savings").

**"Stranger danger" appears once**, in FAQ 4: "reinforce at home by **discussing
stranger danger without scaring them**". The words **abduction**, **kidnap**,
**assault** and **predator** appear **nowhere on the page**, which is worth
recording as a positive: the page sells on confidence and fitness rather than on
parental fear, which is the standard failure mode of this product category.

#### d) Psychological claims

Ordinary confidence copy throughout — "self esteem **rise fast**", "**less
shyness**", "**quiet assurance**", "stay prepared **without constant worry**",
"**mental toughness**". Nothing clinical in the body.

**But FAQ 5 cites neuroscience for a parenting technique:** the "9 minute rule"
answer ends "It's based on **affective neuroscience** and aims to strengthen
parent child bonds". FAQ 7 attributes the screen-time rule to "**psychiatrist
Serge Tisseron**" — the only named authority anywhere on this site, and he has
nothing to do with martial arts.

#### e) Ages, and the fourth programme name for the same class

| Source | Bands |
|---|---|
| `/kids-self-defense-classes-near-me/` | **Little Warriors (Ages 4–6)**, **Junior Defense Program (Ages 7–10)**, **Pre Teen & Teen Program (Ages 11–15)** |
| `/anti-bullying-martial-arts-for-kids/` | Young Kids 4–6, Junior 7–10, Pre Teen and Teen 11–15 |
| `/` (homepage) | Kids **4 to 12**; **Little Champions 4 to 7**; Rising Champions |
| `/kids-bjj-classes/` | **no ages stated** |

**The bands match the bullying page exactly** — the two were published three
minutes apart from the same template — **and neither matches the programme.** But
this page introduces a **new name for the 4–6 group: "Little Warriors".** The
homepage calls it Little Champions. The bullying page calls it Young Kids and then
uses the words "little champions" inside the paragraph. **That is three names for
one class across three pages**, and a parent who rings up asking to enrol in
"Little Warriors" will be asking for something the gym does not call anything.

**FAQ 2 adds a fourth set of ages**, with hedging: "as early as **4 years old**…
By age **5** kids are often ready for more structured classes… Some experts suggest
**waiting until 6-7** for full engagement". The page therefore advertises a 4–6
class and then quotes unnamed experts recommending against starting before 6.

#### f) Parents, teachers and police

**Police are never mentioned. Teachers are never mentioned. Telling an adult is
never named as a step.** The words "police", "911", "emergency services",
"trusted adult", "tell a parent" and "tell a teacher" appear **zero times** in the
body.

The page says "**get help**" twice and "yell run to safety and **get help**" once,
without ever saying from whom. Parents appear only as customers — they "watch from
viewing areas", "say these choices make training part of the weekly routine" and
get their "**parent questions**" answered at the free trial.

**This is the same gap recorded at issue 86g, now on a second page**, and it is
worse here because this page's threat model is an adult attacker rather than a
playground bully. **The two pages together mean the site has 7,271 words of child
safety advice that never once names a teacher, a parent or the police as someone
to tell.** One shared paragraph would fix both.

**The single clearest emergency instruction on the page is in FAQ 10**, and it is
about leaving a child home alone, not about self defence.

#### g) Images: four identifiable children, two in another academy's branded uniform

| File | What it shows |
|---|---|
| `kids-self-def-01.jpg` (featured) | **Two identifiable children**, roughly 5–9, faces clear, receiving a belt from an instructor; three adults present; white BJJ gis with a circular club mark |
| `kids-self-def-02.jpg` | **About ten children** in a striking class, several faces visible in profile; gym fitted with Muay Thai bags and wall pads; uniforms carry **a triangle logo and coloured sashes that are not this gym's branding** |
| `kids-self-def-03.jpg` | **One identifiable child**, face fully visible and laughing, punching focus mitts held by an adult; her gi reads "**POWER**" with a triangle crest — **another school's branded uniform** |

**Two points for the client, the second one new.**

1. **Photo consent.** At least four identifiable minors appear across the three
   images. **The client should confirm signed photo consent is on file for each**,
   or replace the images. Same requirement as issue 86.
2. **Two of the three show children wearing a different academy's branded kit.**
   Image 3 in particular is a close, cheerful portrait of a child in a uniform
   marked "POWER" — placed directly under the heading "**Certified Instructors
   Experienced in Training Children**", so a parent reads it as this gym's coach
   and this gym's student. Neither appears to be the Coppell facility. This is a
   stock-image problem with a sharper edge than usual: it is a photograph of
   someone else's child, in someone else's club colours, used to illustrate this
   gym's instructors.

#### The FAQ is half parenting trivia, and one answer gives away where it was written

**Five of the ten FAQ answers have nothing to do with self defence, martial arts
or children's safety:**

| FAQ | Question | Subject |
|---|---|---|
| 5 | "What is the 9 minute rule for kids?" | parent-child bonding |
| 7 | "What is the 3 6 9 12 rule for kids?" | **screen time** |
| 8 | "What is the 50 30 20 rule for kids?" | **household budgeting** |
| 9 | "What is the 7 7 7 rule of parenting?" | parenting time |
| 10 | "Can I leave a 7 year old alone for 10 minutes?" | **child supervision law** |

A martial arts school is answering, on a page selling classes, how to split a
child's pocket money three ways. This is the fourth instance of the off-topic-FAQ
pattern (issues 77, 81, 85) and the most extreme: it is not merely off-topic, it is
a different subject area.

**FAQ 10 is worse than off-topic — it is unqualified legal advice, and it leaks**
**the writer's location.** The answer reads, in full:

> Laws vary by location; many U.S. states don't specify an age but guidelines
> suggest children under 8-10 shouldn't be left alone even briefly due to safety
> risks. For a 7 year old, 10 minutes might be okay if they're mature, know
> emergency procedures and have access to a neighbor or phone but it's generally
> not recommended—consider maturity and local laws. **In Pakistan (based on your
> location) there's no strict age law but child protection emphasizes
> supervision**; err on caution and build independence gradually.

**"In Pakistan (based on your location)"** is the AI assistant's own geolocation of
whoever generated the text, pasted into the page and published. It is addressed to
"your location" — so a parent in Coppell, Texas is told what the law is in
Pakistan, on the basis of where they supposedly are.

This is the same class of defect as the AI generation timestamp at issue 70, but
more damaging, because:

- it is **evidence the content was not written by this business**, published in
  the business's own voice;
- it is **on a page about child safety**, where trust is the entire product;
- it gives a **Texas parent legal guidance for a different country** about when a
  seven-year-old may be left alone — a question with real consequences;
- it is **quotable**. A competitor, a reviewer or a local parents' group would need
  one screenshot.

**Reproduced verbatim on the rebuilt page**, per the correct-nothing rule, and
**included in the FAQPage JSON-LD** unlike the lorem-ipsum placeholder at issue 66
— that one was not a question at all, whereas this is a real, answered question
that happens to be wrong. **Recommended fix: delete FAQ answers 5, 7, 8, 9 and 10
entirely.** None of them helps a parent choose a martial arts school and the last
actively harms the page.

### 88. The third child-safety page: a family class that no programme page offers, parents and children paired on the mat, and four FAQ answers about God — CONTENT (accuracy), SAFEGUARDING — **High, the client must confirm before publication**

`/family-martial-arts-classes/` (26 February 2026, 3,275 words) was published
**one minute after** `/kids-self-defense-classes-near-me/` and four minutes after
`/anti-bullying-martial-arts-for-kids/`. It is the **third page in this group**.
Everything below is **quoted exactly as published** and **reproduced verbatim on
the rebuilt page** except the one sentence logged at issue 0.

#### The family class itself: stated as running, in the present tense, throughout

**a) Does the page say family classes currently run, with adults and children
training together?** Yes, unambiguously and repeatedly. "Family class" appears
**8 times**, "train together" **10 times**:

| Exact wording |
|---|
| "welcome kids and adults parents and kids teens and adults and kids and families to **train together in classes for adults and kids**" |
| "**Train together in family class** builds close bonds and teamwork" |
| "Kids martial arts martial arts classes for kids karate classes taekwondo classes and adult martial arts **all happen side by side** so everyone shares the same positive space" |
| "Families with mixed levels can **join together in family class or split into separate classes**" |
| "**Separate classes or combined family class options** let everyone progress at the right level" |
| "Kids little ones and 7 year old children **join children class while families train together** or take separate classes as needed" |
| "help families with mixed levels **join family class** or split as needed" |

**The gym has five programme pages: Adult BJJ, Adult Muay Thai, Kids BJJ, Teen BJJ
and Teen Muay Thai. None of them is a family or mixed-age class.** This is not an
SEO framing of an existing programme: a session where a parent and a seven-year-old
drill together is operationally a different class from an adult class and a kids
class running at different times. It needs its own slot, its own ratio and its own
supervision model.

It also now has history. `/martial-arts-gyms-near-me/` advertised "Special Family
and Group Sessions" and `/jiu-jitsu-near-me/` advertised family discounts, neither
confirmed anywhere. **This is the third page to sell a family offering and the
first to build a whole article on it.** The client must confirm whether the class
exists before this goes live.

**b) Times, days or a schedule?** No. The page has an H2 "Class Schedule and
Program Options" and an H3 "Flexible Timings for Weekdays and Weekends", and the
most specific thing in either is: "**Weekday morning early classes** help busy
parents start the day active **evening sessions** suit after school or after work
hours and **weekend mornings or afternoons** give extra time for longer family
practice." No day, no clock time, no frequency. Compare
`/anti-bullying-martial-arts-for-kids/`, which does publish "the **three to six pm
range**".

**c) Family rate, discount or membership?** **No, and this is the one commercial
restraint on the page.** The words "discount", "family rate", "price", "pricing"
and "cost" (other than "no cost") appear **nowhere**. The only commercial
sentences are "**ask about membership options**" and the free trial. **No figure
appears anywhere on the page.** So this page does **not** join the ten logged at
issue 48: it has no pricing section and makes no pricing promise, which is the
honest way to handle a price you will not publish.

**The free trial is stated clearly and matches the programme pages:** an H3 "Free
Trial Class for Families", "Try Family Martial Arts Classes **with no cost through
our free trial class for families**", "Come **as a group** parents and kids
together", "**No pressure to sign up** right away". The group framing is new — the
programme pages offer an individual free class — so even the trial needs
confirming.

**Private family training is advertised again** — an H3 "**Private Family Training
Sessions**", "**one on one time with sensei**", "**You set the time day and
focus**". No price, no booking route, no programme page. See issue 73; this is the
fourth recent page.

**d) Age range for children?** **None is stated.** There is no minimum age, no
maximum and no band. The only age markers are "**7 year old children**" (6 times)
and "**little ones**" (9 times). It therefore does not contradict
`/kids-bjj-classes/` — it says nothing to contradict it with. But set against its
two same-day siblings, which both publish **4–6 / 7–10 / 11–15**, the silence is
its own problem: three pages published within four minutes of each other give a
parent three different answers about who may attend, one of which is no answer.

The FAQ pulls in the opposite direction entirely: **two of the ten answers are
about starting martial arts at 70** ("People begin in their **70s and 80s**",
"Good instructors modify classes, **use chairs for support**"). On a page about
families with seven-year-olds, the age guidance given is for pensioners.

**e) Disciplines named for family classes, and which lack a programme page:**

| Discipline | Named how | Programme page? |
|---|---|---|
| Jiu jitsu / Brazilian jiu jitsu | in the repeated list, 7 times | **yes** (adult, teen, kids BJJ) |
| **Karate** | "**karate classes**", "fun karate style strikes", in the list 7 times | **no** |
| **Taekwondo** | "**taekwondo classes**", "taekwondo high kicks", in the list 7 times | **no** |
| **Gracie** | "karate taekwondo jiu jitsu brazilian jiu jitsu **gracie** and more", 4 times | **no** — a lineage/brand name used as a style |
| Muay Thai | **never mentioned** | n/a |

"**Karate classes taekwondo classes**" is stated as a thing the school runs, four
separate times, in the present tense. The instructor claim goes further: "They hold
**proper training in karate taekwondo jiu jitsu brazilian jiu jitsu** and family
teaching methods". Added to the service-claims entry at issue 39.

**The page also calls its instructors "sensei"** (6 times) and refers to the gym as
a "**dojo**" (4 times) — Japanese karate terminology for a Brazilian jiu jitsu and
Muay Thai academy. Minor, but it is the same tell as the discipline list: the copy
was not written about this gym.

#### Mixed-age training: described explicitly, supervised in general terms, with no pairing rule

**Adults and children are described training and drilling together, five times:**

| Exact wording |
|---|
| "**Parents and kids pair up for drills** cheer each other and **practice moves side by side**" |
| "Parents and kids **pair up for drills hold pads for each other**" |
| "**lets moms dads and children pair up hold pads** cheer each other and laugh through drills" |
| "Families mix levels too with **parents sometimes helping kids or learning alongside them**" |
| "Sensei create moments where **families practice side by side**" |

**Is supervision mentioned? Yes, but only in general terms.** "Instructors watch
closely enforce proper form" (the rest of that sentence is the omitted claim at
issue 0) and "rules that keep everyone protected during punch kicks and punches
grappling and partner work".

**Is there any age or size pairing rule? No.** The nearest the page comes is
"Instructors **group by skill and age groups**" and "Instructors **place everyone
correctly**" — both about which *class* a student joins, not who they are paired
with inside it. Contrast `/kids-self-defense-classes-near-me/`, published a minute
earlier, which does state one: "They **pair kids by size age and skill**".

**So the page describes an adult and a child drilling striking and grappling
together, with no stated rule governing the pairing.** That is the safeguarding
point for the client. Parent-and-child pad work is ordinary and safe when run
properly; what is missing is any published statement of how it is run — whether
children only ever pair with their own parent, whether an unrelated adult may ever
be paired with someone else's child, and what the supervision ratio is.
**Nothing has been added to the rebuilt page**; the gap is recorded here.

**This is the third page in a row with the same structural omission.** Issues 86g
and 87f record that neither earlier page names a teacher, parent or the police as
someone to tell. This one never states a pairing rule. All three were published
within four minutes of each other, from the same template, and none was reviewed
by anyone who runs children's classes.

#### g) Images: about ten identifiable children, in a competing franchise's uniform, and one photo reused from another post

| File | What it shows |
|---|---|
| `family-classes-01.jpg` (featured) | **About ten identifiable children**, faces clear, in a **taekwondo** class. The doboks carry visible **ATA** patches — the American Taekwondo Association, a competing franchise organisation |
| `family-classes-02.jpg` | A large **taekwondo** class, roughly thirty **adults** in doboks and black belts — **no children at all**, on a page about families |
| `family-classes-03.jpg` | **The same photograph already published as `anti-bullying-03.jpg`** — an adult karate class in a traditional dojo, re-captioned "Beginner Intermediate and Advanced Levels" |

**Four points for the client.**

1. **Photo consent.** The featured image shows roughly ten identifiable minors.
   **The client should confirm signed photo consent is on file**, or replace it.
   Same requirement as issues 86 and 87.
2. **A competitor's branding is on the featured image.** The ATA patch is legible
   at full size. This gym's main page for families leads with a photograph of
   another organisation's students in that organisation's uniform.
3. **None of the three images shows the disciplines this gym teaches.** All three
   are taekwondo or karate. No BJJ, no Muay Thai, no Coppell facility — on a page
   whose body also sells karate and taekwondo classes (above), so the images and
   the copy are consistently wrong together.
4. **One image is used twice across two posts with different captions**, which is
   the pattern logged at issue 26. Here the same adult karate class illustrates
   "Beginner to Advanced Levels" on one page and "Beginner Intermediate and
   Advanced Levels" on another.

#### The FAQ: four answers about God, one about Barack Obama, and ADHD again

**Four of the ten FAQ answers are religious or theological**, on a commercial page
selling classes to families:

| FAQ | Question | What the answer contains |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | "**Does God approve of martial arts?**" | 1 Timothy 4:8, King David, denominational positions on "Eastern spiritual elements" |
| 5 | "**Which sin will God never forgive?**" | **Mark 3:28–29, Matthew 12:31–32, blasphemy against the Holy Spirit, repentance and salvation** |
| 6 | "**Was Jesus a martial artist?**" | the money changers, "turn the other cheek", "mainstream scholars see no support" |
| 7 | "Does Barack Obama know martial arts?" | a named political figure's exercise habits |

**FAQ 5 is the one to act on first.** "Which sin will God never forgive?" has no
connection to martial arts, to families or to this business. The answer is a short
piece of Christian doctrine on the unforgivable sin, published in the gym's own
voice, on a page aimed at parents in a religiously mixed suburb. Whatever the
client's own faith, a martial arts school telling prospective customers which sins
God will not forgive is a reputational exposure with no upside, and it will be the
first thing anyone screenshots.

**FAQ 3 repeats the ADHD pattern** logged at issue 86d: "karate can be **very good
for kids with ADHD**", "improve attention **impulse control and self regulation**",
"Many parents **and studies** note" — with no study named. That is now **three**
pages making clinical claims about ADHD with no source.

**Only two of the ten answers are about anything this gym sells.** The rest cover
theology, a US president, starting at 70 (twice), the "4 main martial arts" and
"the hardest martial art". This is the fifth instance of the off-topic-FAQ pattern
(issues 77, 81, 85, 87).

**Recommended fix: delete FAQ answers 1, 5, 6 and 7 before anything else on this
page.**

### 89. A Muay Thai FAQ in which three answers teach the Thai language, including what "555" means in Thai slang — SEO, CONTENT — **High**

`/muay-thai-classes-coppell/` closes with nine FAQ questions. **Three of them are
about the Thai language**, not about Muay Thai:

| # | Question | Subject |
|---|---|---|
| 5 | "**Can I learn Thai in 3 months?**" | learning to speak Thai — "greetings numbers, simple sentences, directions, **food orders**" |
| 7 | "**What does 555 mean in Thai slang?**" | "555 means haha or laughing out loud because the number 5 in Thai is pronounced ha" |
| 9 | "**Is English or Thai harder to learn?**" | "Thai has **five tones** … a unique script with **44 consonants**" |

**The cause is obvious and worth naming for the client:** whoever assembled the
FAQ pulled "people also ask" results for the word **Thai** and did not notice that
most of them are about Thailand and the Thai language rather than about Muay Thai.
FAQ 7 is the clearest proof — "what does 555 mean in Thai slang" is a tourist's
question, answered in full, on a Coppell gym's class page.

Counting FAQ 1 (which answers with other gyms' prices, issue 48), **four of the
nine answers are about something other than training at this gym**. The five that
are on topic are good: starting age, jiu jitsu versus Muay Thai, training three
times a week, Muay Thai versus kickboxing, and the 15/30/15 interval method.

This is the sixth instance of the off-topic-FAQ pattern (issues 77, 81, 85, 87,
88) and the most mechanically explicable: the other five were padded with
celebrity or parenting trivia, this one with a **keyword homonym**.

**Reproduced verbatim and included in the FAQPage JSON-LD**, because each is a
real question with a real answer — unlike the lorem-ipsum placeholder at issue 66,
which was excluded. **Recommended fix: delete answers 5, 7 and 9**, and replace
FAQ 1 with this gym's actual rates.

### 90. The fourth child-safety page: children sparring in headgear, a minimum age the page states two ways, and four FAQ answers about UFC fighters — CONTENT (accuracy), SAFEGUARDING — **High, the client must confirm before publication**

`/mma-classes-for-kids/` (26 February 2026, 3,851 words) is the **fourth page in
this group** and the first to sell a full combat sport to children as its whole
subject. Everything below is **quoted exactly as published** and **reproduced
verbatim on the rebuilt page** except the three sentences at issue 0.

#### Claims that training keeps a child safe

**No outright guarantee survives** — the three that came closest were omitted
(issue 0). What remains is qualified and was kept:

- "We require full protective gear **so kids stay safe** during all activities"
- "we make sure every kid **train safely** enjoys the fun and exciting drills"
- "children **always stay safely on the mat** with proper protective gear and glove"
- "Children learn self defense basics that teach them to **defend stay calm and get
  away if needed**"
- "Parents share stories of … the **ability to defend themselves if ever needed**"

#### Children sparring and striking: described in detail, at every age band

**Yes, extensively.** This is the most contact-heavy children's page on the site:

| Age band | Exact wording |
|---|---|
| All | "Kids start with **simple striking moves from boxing and kickboxing**. They learn how to **throw clean punches** use proper footwork and **add kicks** with good form" |
| 4–6 | "Children learn **basic striking like safe punches and kicks** grapple holds that feel like wrestling with friends" — but also "Everything stays gentle **no hard contact**" |
| 7–10 | "They work on **clean boxing punches kickboxing kicks muay thai style knees and wrestling takedowns**… partner work and **light spar with rules**" |
| 11–15 | "Classes combine **full contact style drills** with control so everyone stays safely protected by glove and gear" |
| Advanced | "Advanced kids move to tougher combinations **submission chains light spar** and strategy from ufc bellator and pro fights examples" |

**"Full contact style drills" for eleven-year-olds is the line the client should
look at first.** It is hedged ("style", "with control"), but it is the phrase a
worried parent or a journalist would quote.

#### Supervision, protective equipment and contact rules: all three are present, and this is the page's strongest section

Unlike issues 86, 87 and 88, **this page does state its rules**:

| Measure | Exact wording |
|---|---|
| Equipment | "Children wear **headgear mouthguards shin guards chest protectors and glove sized just for them**" |
| Contact limit | "**no full power strikes light contact only** and always with coach watching closely" |
| Contact limit | "Strict rules **no full contact unless ready** proper tap signals" |
| Supervision | "Every class has enough coach and instructor to watch every child closely **no one gets left unsupervised even for a second**" |
| Pairing rule | "**pair kids of similar size and skill** for partner work" |
| Stop rule | "Instructor **stop drills right away if anything looks off**" |
| Tap rule | "Kids learn to **tap early** to show when they need a break" |
| Modification | "If a child feels tired sore or unsure we **give rest modify moves or switch to lighter drills**" |

**That is a genuinely good set, and it is worth saying so.** A size-and-skill
pairing rule is exactly what `/family-martial-arts-classes/` was missing (issue
88). The one gap is **head strikes**: the body never says whether strikes to the
head are permitted, while the page's own FAQ says good programmes "limit head
strikes". On a kids MMA page that needs stating in the body.

#### Ages: the page contradicts itself, and contradicts the programme

| Source | Minimum | Maximum |
|---|---|---|
| This page, opening and twice more | "**Kids ages 6 and up**" | — |
