| This page, age-group section | "**Little Champions Program (Ages 4 6)** … welcomes very young kids **ages 4 to 6**" | "**Youth MMA Program (Ages 11 15)**" |
| This page, FAQ 1 | "as young as **4 or 5**"; "**Experts say** formal full MMA with grappling and striking often starts around **8 to 13**"; "Some places wait until **13-16**" | — |
| `/kids-bjj-classes/` + homepage | **4**, Little Champions **4 to 7** | **12** |

**Three conflicts:**

1. **The page states its own minimum age two different ways.** "Ages 6 and up"
   appears three times, including in the opening sentence and the closing
   curriculum paragraph; "ages 4 to 6" appears in the age-group section. A parent
   of a five-year-old gets both answers on one page.
2. **Little Champions is 4–7 on the homepage and 4–6 here**, and here it is an MMA
   programme rather than a BJJ one (issue 39). This is the second page to reband
   it — `/anti-bullying-martial-arts-for-kids/` used 4–6 as well.
3. **The maximum runs to 15**, where the kids programme ends at 12 and teens are
   served by `/teen-bjj-classes/` and `/teen-muay-thai-classes/`.

**The page's own FAQ undercuts its youngest programme.** FAQ 1 says experts put
formal MMA with grappling and striking at "**around 8 to 13**" and that some gyms
"wait until 13-16". The page sells it from 4.

#### Images: fifteen or so identifiable children, and the third reuse of one photograph

| File | What it shows |
|---|---|
| `mma-kids-01.jpg` (featured) | About **ten identifiable children** in a striking class — **the same photograph already published as `kids-self-def-02.jpg`** on `/kids-self-defense-classes-near-me/`, re-captioned |
| `mma-kids-02.jpg` | **Five identifiable young children**, faces full-frame and smiling, arms round each other — in **karate gis with white and yellow belts**, not MMA |
| `mma-kids-03.jpg` | An adult karate class — **the same photograph published as `anti-bullying-03.jpg` and `family-classes-03.jpg`**, now on its **third** page under a third caption |

**Three points:**

1. **Photo consent.** Roughly fifteen identifiable minors across two images, and
   `mma-kids-02.jpg` is the closest, clearest portrait set of children anywhere in
   this audit. **The client should confirm signed photo consent is on file**, or
   replace them. Same requirement as issues 86, 87 and 88.
2. **One photograph is now on three pages.** The adult karate class illustrates
   "Beginner to Advanced Levels", "Beginner Intermediate and Advanced Levels" and
   now "Beginner and Advanced Programs". See issue 26.
3. **No image shows MMA.** Two are karate, one is a striking class in another
   school's uniform. None is the Coppell facility.

#### The FAQ: four answers about professional fighters, one about a UFC bonus cheque

| FAQ | Question | Subject |
|---|---|---|
| 6 | "At what age did **Conor McGregor** start fighting?" | a pro fighter's career |
| 8 | "What age did **Jon Jones** start fighting?" | a pro fighter's career |
| 9 | "Is **25 too late to start UFC**?" | an adult pro-career question |
| 10 | "Who won the **$300 000 bonus at UFC 300**?" | "Max Holloway won two for his **knockout of Justin Gaethje** earning $600 000" |

**Four of ten answers on a page for parents of four-to-fifteen-year-olds are about
professional cage fighting**, and the last one describes a knockout and a prize
cheque. The body of the page works hard to say this is about discipline and
confidence rather than fighting — "the focus stays on learning not fighting hard",
"without making them aggressive" — and then the FAQ tells a parent how much Max
Holloway was paid for a knockout.

This is the seventh instance of the off-topic-FAQ pattern (issues 77, 81, 85, 87,
88, 89). **Recommended fix: delete answers 6, 8, 9 and 10.** The six that remain
are genuinely useful, and **FAQ 1 and FAQ 3 are the best child-safety writing on
the site** — they should be promoted into the body, not buried in an accordion.

#### FAQ 4 publishes prices, and they are not this gym's

"How much does jiu jitsu cost for kids?" answers with "**$100 and $220 per
month**", "**$150 to $200**", "**$120** in smaller towns", "**$180 to $250**" and
one-off fees "**around $50 to $200**", closing with "**check local gyms for exact
rates**". These are US market averages, not Sixth Sense's, and the page's own
"Affordable Membership Plans" section names no price. Same defect as
`/muay-thai-classes-coppell/` (issue 48), and here the question is about **jiu
jitsu** on a page selling **MMA**.

### 91. Six pages tell the Muay Thai origin story and they disagree with each other on dates, kings and titles — CONTENT (accuracy) — **High**

`/history-of-muay-thai/` (8 March 2026) is the sixth page covering Muay Thai and
Muay Boran origins. It is the most organised of them — a clean era-by-era
structure — and it **contradicts its siblings on three substantive points and
itself on a fourth**. Everything below is quoted exactly and reproduced verbatim.

#### What this page states

**Dates and periods:**

| Claim | Exact wording |
|---|---|
| Sukhothai | "**Sukhothai Era (1238–1438)**"; FAQ: "began in the Sukhothai Kingdom around the **13th century**" |
| Ayutthaya | "**Ayutthaya Era (1350–1767)**" |
| Rattanakosin | "**Rattanakosin Era (1782–Present)**" |
| Nai Khanom Tom | body: "captured during war in the **late 1700s**"; FAQ 8: "a Thai fighter in the **18th century**" |
| Stadiums | "Rajadamnern opened first in **1941** and Lumpinee followed in **1956**" |
| IFMA | "known as IFMA formed in **1993**" |
| IOC | "IFMA gained provisional recognition from the IOC in **2016** and full recognition later" |
| Modernisation | "Modern Muay Thai took shape in the **20th century**" |
| Global spread | "**After World War II** Muay Thai gained huge international popularity" |
| Age of the art | FAQ 4: "Muay Thai traces back to ancient Thailand **over 500 years ago**" |

**Named figures:** **Nai Khanom Tom** ("widely regarded as the father of Muay
Thai"), **King Prachao Sua (The Tiger King)** ("He ruled in the Ayutthaya Era and
trained in Muay Thai himself. The king often **fought in disguise** at local
matches"), **King Rama V** ("played a major role in making Muay Thai a national
symbol… worked with the **sport authority of Thailand**"), the **Burmese** army,
and the **Sport Authority of Thailand**.

**No source, reference, historian or citation appears anywhere on the page.** The
words "source", "according to", "historian" and "research" occur zero times.

#### The contradictions across the cluster

**1. Nai Khanom Tom is given three different centuries on three pages.**

| Page | When |
|---|---|
| `/history-of-muay-thai/` body | "the **late 1700s**" |
| `/history-of-muay-thai/` FAQ 8 | "the **18th century**" |
| `/muay-boran-techniques/` | "defeating multiple Burmese champions in **1774**"; "**18th century**" |
| `/are-there-multiple-styles-of-muay-boran/` | "who in the **16th century** defeated Burmese opponents" |

**The 16th-century claim is roughly two hundred years adrift** of the other three
and of the generally accepted date. One of the four pages is wrong and a reader
comparing any two of them can see it.

**2. He is the father of a different art depending on the page.** This page: "Nai
Khanom Tom is widely regarded as **the father of Muay Thai**". 
`/muay-boran-techniques/`: "**There is no single “father”**, but Nai Khanom Tom
… is widely regarded as the legendary **father of Muay Boran** (and Muay Thai)".

**3. Two different kings are credited with modernising the sport.**

| Page | Who modernised it |
|---|---|
| `/history-of-muay-thai/` | "**King Rama V** … helped set rules for safer matches… turned [it] into an official national sport" |
| `/muay-boran/` | "Later leaders like **King Rama VII** modernized the Thai martial arts… Around **King Rama VII's** time changes started happening" |
| `/are-there-multiple-styles-of-muay-boran/` | "modernization under **King Rama VII** introduced Western influences like boxing rings, gloves, referees, weight classes, and timed rounds" |

Two pages give the gloves-and-rings reform to Rama VII; this one gives the
rule-setting and national promotion to Rama V. They may both be partly right, but
nothing on any of the three pages reconciles them, and
`/are-there-multiple-styles-of-muay-boran/` separately refers to "**King
Chulalongkorn V**" — who **is** Rama V — as a different figure in a different
role, so that page names the same king twice under two names.

**4. The page contradicts itself on how old the art is.** The body dates the
origin to **1238** (about 790 years). FAQ 4 says "**over 500 years ago**".

#### Regional styles: this page names none

**Muay Korat, Muay Chaiya, Muay Lopburi, Muay Thasao, Mae Mai and Look Mai appear
zero times.** So there is no transliteration conflict to report from this page —
but it is the site's designated history article and it omits the regional-style
story that four sibling pages are built on. The existing inconsistencies in that
story therefore stand uncorrected, including the "**Muay Lopbur**" misspelling on
`/are-there-multiple-styles-of-muay-boran/`.

**The Muay Boran predecessor claim does match the cluster**, which is the one
point of agreement: "**Muay Boran is the ancient form of Muay Thai** that Thai
warriors practiced long ago" sits comfortably with `/muay-boran/`'s "Muay boran
holds a special place as **the predecessor**" and `/muay-boran-techniques/`'s
"**ancestor of modern Muay Thai**".

#### Cultural and religious claims, for client review

These are live practices, so an inaccurate statement is a credibility problem with
practitioners. **Reproduced verbatim, nothing altered:**

- "Fighters perform **Wai Kru Ram Muay** before every match to pay respect to
  teachers parents and ancestors. The **Ram Muay** dance shows personal style and
  focus."
- "Fighters wear **Mongkol** on their heads and **Prajioud** on their arms as
  **sacred items**. These ceremonies start with music from traditional Thai
  instruments."
- FAQ 2, "Is Muay Thai Buddhist?": "Muay Thai is **not a religion** but it has
  strong ties to Buddhism because Thailand is mostly Buddhist… This ritual comes
  from **Thai Buddhist and animist traditions**. Many fighters pray or make
  offerings at temples for protection and strength."

**Two notes for whoever reviews this.** The page spells it "**Wai Kru**" six times
and never "Wai Khru", which is the more common transliteration and the spelling
used in the brief; and "before **every** match" is stated absolutely where
practice varies by promotion and ruleset. Neither is altered.

#### Recommendation

**Pick one set of dates and one lineage story, then make all six pages agree.**
The specific decisions needed are: which century Nai Khanom Tom fought in, whether
he is the father of Muay Thai or Muay Boran or neither, and whether Rama V or Rama
VII modernised the sport. Until those three are settled, every page in the cluster
undermines the other five, and a reader who checks two of them finds the site
contradicting itself on its own subject.

### 92. A fitness page that names three medical conditions and cites "studies" three times without naming one — CONTENT (accuracy), SEO — **High**

`/benefits-of-kickboxing/` (8 March 2026) was checked against the same categories
as `/benefits-of-muay-thai/`, built the day before from the same template. **The
Muay Thai page came back clean on every one. This one does not.**

#### Three claims about named medical conditions

| # | Exact wording | Condition named |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | "This aerobic and anaerobic exercise **lowers the risk of heart disease** and boosts cardiovascular endurance" | **heart disease** |
| 2 | "Kickboxing **may help with mobility even for people with multiple sclerosis** according to some pilot study findings" | **multiple sclerosis** |
| 3 | FAQ 6: "**Studies on high impact activities show they help maintain or improve bone density** especially in adults" | **bone density / bone health** |

**The multiple sclerosis claim is the one to act on.** A commercial gym page is
telling people with a progressive neurological condition that its classes may help
their mobility, sourced to "**some pilot study findings**" that are not named,
dated or linked. Whether or not such a pilot exists, a reader with MS cannot check
it, and the sentence sits in a sales page rather than a clinical one. It is the
only claim in the whole audit that addresses a diagnosed neurological condition.

**"Lowers the risk of heart disease" is a disease-prevention claim** stated flatly,
with no hedge and no source. Contrast the Muay Thai page, which made the same
point correctly: "Your heart gets stronger from the constant elevated heart rate"
and "brings your resting heart rate down" — improvement, not prevention.

#### Three research claims, no source for any

| Where | Exact wording |
|---|---|
| Benefit 3 | "**Studies suggest** that kickboxing three days per week brings significant improvements in heart health for most people" |
| Benefit 6 | "**according to some pilot study findings**" |
| FAQ 6 | "**Studies on high impact activities show**…" |

"Studies", "study" and "according to" appear three times between them and **not
one study, author, journal or year is named**. This joins the uncited figures
already logged on the kids pages (issue 86c), the women's page (issue 87c and the
one-in-three / one-in-five statistics) and the Muay Thai benefits page's
afterburn claim. **Logged at MEDIUM with that group**, with the MS sentence
escalated above.

#### No numbers, and no conflict with the Muay Thai page

**Neither benefits page publishes a single calorie or weight figure**, so there is
no contradiction to report between them:

| | `/benefits-of-muay-thai/` | `/benefits-of-kickboxing/` |
|---|---|---|
| Calorie figure | none | none |
| Weight-loss figure | none | none |
| Timeframe | "after a few months" | none |
| Frequency | none | "three times a week", "three days per week" |
| Afterburn | "creates an **afterburn effect**" | "create a strong **after burn** effect" |

Both rely on the same unquantified afterburn claim, spelled two different ways.
That is the one point where the twins agree and both are vague, which is
preferable to agreeing on a number that is wrong — but it means neither page
supports the weight-loss promise in its own meta description with anything a
reader can act on.

#### Mental health

**This page puts it in the H1**: "Top 10 Benefits of Kickboxing for Fitness
Strength and **Mental Health**", with benefit 8 "Reduces Stress and **Improves
Mental Health**". The supporting text is ordinary stress-relief writing —
"triggers **endorphin release** which lifts mood and leaves you feeling calm",
"help clear your mind and improve mental focus" — and **no clinical condition is
named**: "anxiety" and "depression" appear zero times.

So the claim is defensible in the body and overstated in the heading. "Improves
Mental Health" as an H1 and H2 promises more than endorphins and focus, and it is
the string Google will show. **Reproduced exactly**; the fix is one word, "Mental
Wellbeing" or "Mental Focus".

#### No disclaimer

**The page carries no medical or fitness disclaimer of any kind.** On a page that
names heart disease, multiple sclerosis and bone density, that is a larger gap
than it was on the Muay Thai page, which named no condition at all. It joins the
disclaimer group at issue 31 — and unlike the Muay Thai entry, not at the low end.

## How these were checked

- **Spam, phone number, empty headings:** scanned the raw HTML of all 104 pages.
- **FAQ comparisons:** extracted every question and answer from each page and
  compared them word for word.
- **Links:** followed each one and compared its link text with its target.
- **Image sizes:** compared each file's real pixel size with the size the page
  declares.
- **Counts** (placeholders, duplicated headings, typos): taken from the live
  page source, not estimated.
